Death of Mr. James Woolley.

Wp record with regret the death of Mr. James Woolley, which took place at his residence, 353, Liverpool-road, on Wednesday night of last week. The deceased was 68 years of age, and had been employed at Messrs. Monks, Hall and Company's works for a considerable number of years. Earning the confidence of his employers he gradually improved his position, and eventually was appointed day manager. The funeral took place at the Cemetery on Sunday, when there were many evidences of the respect and esteem in which the deceased was held. A large 'limber of the employes of Messrs. Monks, Hall and Company attended and preceded the cortege to the Cemetery. There was also a large attendance etheeea NsV°rikvins closely gmens Mission, Bank Quay, associated. A good iotfi mwhemicbhetrhs of edf ed a many floral tributes were received, including several from the employes.

WOMEN'S CHAT.

The Golden Rose.—The Golden Rose which the Pope has presented to Princess Ens on the occasion of her I marritwe to the King of Spain, recalls the fact that no British Princess has been its recipient since the reign of Queen Mary. It is a mimic plant of pure gold, in a golden pot, upon which is emblazoned the Papal Arms, and its value is not less than £3OO. The leaves are set wEh tiny jewels in imitation of dewdrops, and in the central bud is a receptacle in which there is a small palm leaf, blessed by the Pope. The Golden Rose is the highest honour the Pope can confer upon a Catholic Princess, and it is only presented to those deemed specially worthy of the Vatican's favour. The Spanish Royal Wedding.—The marriage of King ' Alfonso and Princess Ena is to take place on lay 31st, but the wedding festivities will extend to altogether over a week. The superstition that marriages are unlucky at certain seasons of the year, exists nearly everywhere, and although the particular month may not be the same in different countries, many will think that Princess Rua is a little bold in becoming a May bride. The accompanying festivities will include gala banquets a ball, a performance at the Opera, and a Royal bull- fight. Spain is still a great country for bull fights, but it can hardly be expected that Princess Ena will share the national enthusiasm for this form of entertainment. During the past few weeks Princess Ens has had a busy time fitting on the robes and dresses for the great , Court functions at Madrid. Some have been made in I Spain,anel the Princess has also received visits from thel Spanish Court jeweller, to fit the newly made crowns on the head of Spain's future Queen.—A limited number of invitations were issued to view the many magnificent presents at Kensington Palace, which Princess Ena has received, but these are now being packed for the I journey to Spain. Princess Henry of Battenberg, and Princess Ens, are expected at Madrid about the 26th of May, the Prince and Princess of Wales following a day or two later. Buckinghamshire Lace.—Among the wedding presents received by Princess Ena of Battenberg was a'beautiful scarf of Buckinghamshire lace presented by' the North Bucks and Bedfordshire Lace Association, which has clone much to revive the demand for " Bucks pillow points." Ever since the sixteenth century, this cottage industry has been followed in the little old-world villages, but it was in danger of becoming almost a lost art till a few years ago, when a number of ladies under distinguished Royal patronage set about reviving this work. The Association will hold its annual sale on June 7th and Bth, under the auspices of the Duchess of Buckingham, and it will be opened by Mrs. White- law Reid. the wife of the American Ambassador, whose country seat is Bedfordshire. Summer Gowns.—The wise woman has her very-fairy slimmer gowns well under order, if indeed they are not niready completed, for she knows full well that with the crid of May comes a rush to the dressmakers,when orders cannot be so caref ally and thoughtfully carried out as during the slacker part of the season. The painted muslins, which are in great demand among those who can afford such luxuries, are exceedingly beautiful, but the printed varieties—to the uninitiated—look almost as well, and make up charmingly. The economical should see to it however. that the blouse and skirt of the muslin is made entirely separate from the slip, when it can very easily bowashed and ironed at home. Further -the girl or woman who " gets up" her linen and muslin frocks, should consider the style and trimmings from an ironers point of view. By the way, a plain dfail -board, covered tightly with an old blanket, is most necessary in the ironing of skirts, and a quite small ironic desirable for those parts of the blouse that cannot well be reached with irons of the ordinary size.

The Expensive Elhow Sleeve.—The continued rage for the elbow sleeve has brought many novelties in gloves, but it cannot be said of any of them that they make for economy. There are the new short kid -varieties with lace tops, the lace and kid matching oxactly in colour, but-these are little, if any, cheaper than all-kid gloves and most certainly they do not wear or clean as well. Long mittens, it is predicted, will have a great, vogue later on, but it is donbtfnl whether the really " well dressed " will patronise the idea. There are, as usual, maw " lines "of washing doeskin gloves. tut if they resemtle all previous " lines,"-they will certainly do nothing of the kind. About Boas.—The woman who is unable to afford a really good, and full ostrich feather boa, would do well to invest in just the " rna "of ostrich feathers, which does not quite meet in frost, and ends in long streamers of ribbon velvet. This " rufr placed well back, forms a delightful and most becoming frame to the face, and is much to be preferred to the cheap long boas, built of wild and fearsome-looking feallers that have evidently been culled from the farsaylfrd. These cheap boas quickly get .out of order, when they mar the prettiest and daintiest toilette imaginable. Flowers Real and Artiflcial.—At the moment cherries are in great favour for simple hats, and very becoming they prove tG a fresh young face. The woman who has passed her youth should select other fruits of the earth, wherewith to adorn her headgear. The flower of the season is unquestionably again the rose, it is indeed roses, roses, everywhere. Leading nurserymen tell the same tale "ramblers " being in the greatest demand for the table, and the decoration of rooms. Orchids, they say, are fashionable," especially the variety known as the " Alexandra," but many people who flna this partcniar form of decoration too costly, are patronising the iris, which often needs an expert to decide as to whether it is not an orchid after all.

Women and Temperance Reform. Lady Carlisle said at the annual meeting of the National British Women's Temperance Association, that by the next General Election she fully expected that every 'British woman present would have a vote. That expectation may prove to be too sanguine, but there is no doubt that Lady Carlisle is right in her view that the extension of the Parliamentary vote to women would give a great impetus to Temperance Reform. That hen been the experience in Australasia, where Tasmania has recently .afforded another example. In some of the States, women have long enjoyed the franchise, Fred all are intended to vote at the Federal elections, but this year was the first time that women in Tasmania had a vote for their own Parliament. The result is an overwhelm- ing majority, pledged to active measures of Temperance Reform, and a new Licensing Bill, providing for local option, is forthwith to be passed.

MADGE.

FOOTBALL.

Meeting of the Northern Union League

A meeting of the Northern Rugby League Committee was held at the Grosvenor Hotel, Manchester, on Tues- day, Mr. Joseph Platt (Oldham) occupying the chair. The following reported players received suspension : J. Richardson, sem (Whim), up to and including Septem- ter 8, kicking in match against Pontefract, April 28 ; H. Feather (Bradford), up to and, including October 20, striking, Broughton Rangers' match, Aptil 30 •, D. DEATH OF A PROMINENT LANCASHIRE WESLEYAN.

The death occurred suddenly on Monday morning, at Bolton, of Mr. Thomas Walker, one of the best-known Wesleyans in Lancashire. Mr. Walker had held every office open to a layman in the Wesleyan Methodist Church. In 1895 he had a census taken of the persons who attended places of worship in Bolton, and so great was his concern for the non-worshippers that he initiated a movement which culminated in thebuilding of the Victoria. Hall, Bolton, and himself gave .£7,000 towards it. He gave £2,000 towards the King's Hall, Bolton's second mission, and a similar amount to the new Wesleyan Ciaurch. in Castle-street. At the time of his death he was the general treasurer of the Home Missions of the Wesleyan Methodist Church and the treasurer of the Lancashire branch of the Children's Home. Mr. Walker, who was in his 73rd year, was the head of the firm of Messrs. William Walker and Son, , ort ners of the Rose Hill Tannery, Bolton. , An inqnest was held on Tuesday at Bolton on the body of Mr. Walker, who was found dead in his bath. 1 Mr. Arthur Noel Walker. son, stated that it was his father's custom to take a warm spray bath in the morn- ing. He was called in the morning by the maid, to whom he gave instructions to pack his bag, as it was his intention to go to Trend. Later, a servant noticed water coming through the kitchen floor, and on opening the bathroom door she saw the deceased in the bath. There was no water in the bath, bnt the warm spray was coming into the room. Hil father appeared to be dead. —Dr. Mallett said death was caused by sodden heart failure.—The jury returned a verdict of " Natural causes—heart failure."

WARRINGTON BOARD OF GUARDIANS

Dr. Braide's Position.

A meeting of the Warrington Board of Guardians was held at the Workhouse on Wednesday, Mr. J. Billing° presiding. There were also present :—Mrs. Clay. Mrs. Wilkinson, Dr. Murray, Messrs. A. J. Jolley, R. Forshaw, G. Thomron, T. H. Ashcroft. R. Bolton, J. Payton, S. Davies, T. ftitchie, M. W. Seton, W. Flannagan, G. Mather, W. Houghton, A. Brooks, J. C. Sutton (clerk), J. F. Awbery (master), and A. Wright (Superintendent of Padgate schools). - HOUSE COMMITTEE.

- THE MEDICAL OFFICER'S POSITION. At a meeting of the House Committee letter from the Local Government Board • dated May Ist relating to Joseph Casey whose death within 24 hours of his admission had been reported to the Board and subsequently referred by the Guardians to I the House Committee for the purpose, was considered and it was unanimously resolved that the committee having heard the explanations of the Medical Officer, the Master and Superintendent Nurse, cannot concur with the view of the Local Government Board that the Master should have sent for the Medical Officer upon Casey's admission. The committee felt that having an I 'experienced Superintendent Nurse at the hospital and I that officer having given her opinion that the case did not require the immediate attendance of the Medical Officer, no blame attached to the master and further that if he had sent for the Medical Officer he would have shown a disregard of the provisions of the Nursing Order 1898 and of the opinion of the Superintendent Nurse which would have been open to just censure. , At a subsequent meeting a letter from the Local Government Board was received asking whether the Guardians had come to an arrangement with Dr. Braide under which he would resign his office as medical j officer of the Workhouse. It was resolved that the. letter be replied to in the negative with an explanatory note as to the routine of the negotiations between Dr. Braide and the Guardians. ! Mr. FORSHAW moved the confirmation of the minutes with the exception of that relating to Dr. Bra ide which he suggested should be referred back to the committee. It would be remembeied that the Local Government Board pointed out that a gratuity not exceeding twice the amount of the annual salary and emoluments could be awarded the doctor on his ceasing to hold office. He thought that the doctor should be given an oppor- tunity of finally accepting or refusing those terms before any further communication was made to the Local Government Board. He did not intend to elaborate the ' matter as he thought that at that stage " The least said the soonest mended." If they adhered to the resolution they would in all probability have a Local Government Board inquiry. Mr. 'Moons said that as chairmito of the House Com- mittee Mr. Forshaw should only propose the adoption of the minutes and not bring in amendments of his own He(Mr. Brooks)formally moped the confirmation of the I minutes and it would now be open to the chairman to bring forward amendments. Mr. THOMPSON seconded. I Mr. FORSHAW then moved: That the letter from the Local Government Board of May 4th be referred I back to the Hones Committee for further consideration with it view to the House Committee again conferring with Dr. Braide as to whether any settlement could be arrived at on the lines suggested in the terms set forth in the letter of the Local Government Board of January 31st. Mr. ASHCROFT said the Board was making itself a laughing stock. Even now they had come to no settled arrangement. Mr. MATHER considered it would only be passing a vote of censure on the committee to refer the matter back. The amendment was not seconded and fell to the ground and the minutes were confirmed. Mr. JOLLEY said the committee were unanimously of the opinion that there should be an explanatory note making it clear that anyfailure to arrive at an amicable settlement was in no way the fault of the Guardians. They deputed to two leading members the work of 'negotiation with Dr. Braid but theirefforts were futile. It vas decided that before replying to the Local Government Board the Clerk should communicate with Dr. Braide asking if he would accept the proposals arrived at by the Board at their last special meeting. The proposals were as follows : (1) That in the opinion of this committee the existing medical treatment of the sick indoor poor is not satis- factory.—(2) That therefore the' Local Government Board be asked to sanction fresh arrangements includ- ing the appointment of a Resident Medical Officer to be responsible for the Medical and Surgical treatment- of the indoor poor, of the indoor staff, and for the training of the nurses. (3) That the Guardians consent to the payment to Dr. Braide, as compensation for loss of office, the maximum amount allowed by law.

DELEGATES TO A CONFERENCE.

It was agreed that in accordance witri-a-i7equest made to the Board the Guardians be represented at qa special conference to be held on May 22nd and 23rd, 1906, at the Guildhall, London, for consideration of the following subjects :—(1) " Children's Courts and Pro- bation Officers," (2) "Remand Homes," (3) "The Free Feeding of School Children." Some discussion took place as to whom the repre- sentatives should be. Three grouns of names were sub- mitted to the meeting :—The Chairman, Mrs. Clay and the Clerk : the Chairman, Mr. Mather and the Clerk ; Mr. Mather, Mrs. Clay and the Clerk. The delegates selected were the Chairman, Mrs. Clay and the Clerk. Mr. MATHER announced his intention of attending the conference at his own expense. NEW TREASURER.

It was resolved : "That the resignation of Mr,John Murray as Treasurer of the .Guardians be accepted and, in pursuance of the applic...t:9n in that behalf made by the Guardians, that Mr. William Skinner, Mr. Murray's successor at Parr's Bank, be appointed Treasurer on the same terms,and that the Local Government be requested to sanction same."

A SON OF EMPIRR

By MORLEY ROBERTS,

Author of "The Western Avernus," " Strong Men and True," "The Earth Mother," "Red Earth," "The Great Jester," ttc,

SYNOPSIS OF PRECEDING CHAPTERS: Madge Gretton, while touring abroad with her mother, becomes acquainted, in an unconventional way, with a certain Capton Richard Blundell, a military man of great reputation as a traveller and linguist, but whose udvancement in the army is hindered by an old feud with one of his superiors. Madge is an ardent Imperialist. She long ngo made up her mind to marry a man such as Blundell. Her mother ; strives to make her respect the conventions, but one morning the girl secretly goes mountaineering , with Blundell, who is interested in her, and she falls in love with him in earnest. She goes to see him a when he departs for Dum-Dum, and he , kisses her. It transpires that his greatest enemy in the Service is Sir Daniel Fielding, the Adjutant.. I General, the father of Madge's closest friend, Annette Fielding. Sir Daniel Fielding's intense I hatred of Blundell prevents the latter from rising in the Service.

thing in the world the result was more important than the machinery. Why were they zealous for religious education? They desired that every boy or girl who left school should leave more attached to religion than when he or she went, confirmed and strengthened in religious faith, and, what was more relevant,in religious practice. If they were zealous for denominational teaching they were zealous because they believed that the principle of denominational teaching should attach children more closely to a denomination and bring them more in the way of zealous religious practice. They had not the slightest objection to the principle of public control, and a teacher's professional career should hrs.:mite independent of his religious belief, but they said that the first concern must be with the child's well-being. In regard to appointing teachers without tests, it meant they were to be appointed without regard to their religines cenvictiens : that was t say, an Agnostic or a Unitarian might be appointed to give Christian teaching. That principle they denounced as absurd, and it appeared to be abandoned altogether, and now it was contended that a teacher should not be asked to subscribe a particular form of belief. The Bishop had told them that be was a manager of a school but that he never dreamt of a teacher being asked to do anything of the kind. No one could any longer maintain that it was pert of the right of a teacher to give religious teaching whether he was fit for it or not, and it was now said that the local authority had a perfect right to e any inquiry it pleased. le eat became of the g •iev- ce which was so tremendously aired at the late electiou ? The change which took T3lace was that the mechanism was removed from a board of managers to the local authority. That would throw a considerable obligation upon Churchmen if the Bill passed, because, though in debate it was con- ceded teachers ought to be appointed with a due regard to their religious convictions, he was not sure whether it was not intended to get rid of the definite character of religions instruction which had hitherto been considered advisable or essential. The Government in two respects were not faithful to the principle of public control. That was to be shut out in violation of the principle of public content, which reqni re .1 that they a ho paid the piper should call the tone. They must not play a tune which was offensive to the Nonconformists—however much they paid, they must not (3111 that particular tune. (Laughter.). If the tune was against the Church of England, the principle he had alluded to was an inalienable right, but if not, then it could be very well set aside. (Laughtere Then with regard to the four-fifths clause that was frankly stated by ?Jr. Asquith to be put forward to please the Roman Catholics. He (Lord Hugh Cecil) was most anxious that our education should satisfy the Roman Catholir a and all other sincere religious persons inthecommunity. But he did protest as an outrage against all the principles of religious equality that a Government should sit down to their legislative work propounding it as one of their rules that they were to try and satiety Roman Catholics, and not make any corresponding effort to satisfy the far larger number who were joined in the Church of England, (Applause.) He believed there were two motives, neither of them very respectable, underlying this desire to conciliate specially and peculiarly Roman Catholic feeling. One of them was the desire on the part of the most extreme Noncon- formists to attack the Church of England and their willingness to make terms with any other body of opinion in order that they might carry out the attack. Another was to avoid a vehement opposition which Roman Catholic representatives in Parliament and Roman Catholics in the country were known to be ' ready to make, and to concede therefore to vigour what they would not in the case of the Church of England concede to reason. Church people must make it pa-. I fectly Clear that while they desired eareestly and even I passionately that all religioos people should have their religious scruples carefully attended to by Parliament andshonld have a system of education set tip that effec• tirely gave them what they desired foe the teaching of their children, they mast have an education settlement that was n3t framed on so partial a principle that the I Nonconformists, Jews and Roman Catholics were con- I sidered while the rights and convictions of the Church of Engle ed were only considered to be set aside.(Applause). That was the principle I objection to it. It might mesa any.bing or nothiag. Tbe question wee: What sort of religioas instruction and what sort oZ Bible irstractioa were they going 1 to give? That was particularly the case in elementary school, when from the water° of the !case they could only teach a• small part of I the Bible, and when everything depen ied on the selec- tion made by the Educe:ion Cornas•ttee. who the teacher was, and what his opinions were. The clause was expressed in a geaeral term—that no catechism o formula distinotive of a religious denomination was to be taught, but practically speaking, that meant the Church Catechism Was eecluded. They could not con- ceive, if they allowed such a wide discretion, why the Church Catechism was to be singled oat £l3 the one thine not to be taught. By excluding that distinctive Cher& teaching they were creating an atmosphere and impres- sion on a child's mind hostile to the system of the Church. It Was presented to him as being Chris- tianity, as being religion. He was acceetomed to think of it as that, but if he heard more at another time with less authority it. seemed to him teat the matter was untrue or subordinate—a sort of superstenctnre which might be accepted but which did cot stand on the same basis as the fundamental tenets he was taught,. (Applause.) Anyth:ng more unfair or vexatious to religious sentiment than that church, buildings, the fruit of so many aspirations and ow:elfish labours should be used for the purpose they were meant to be used for and should be teroed to a purpose for which theyirrei:sßill as wereßi nowtbpuailsts,ed in could not fm t imagined. f he contended, be paving the way to that Secularism which was sometimes held out as rebogey to drive them

to undenominationalism. He did not believe the British people would, with their eyes open, adopt Secularism, but he thought they might very readily drift into it. If they got the schools managed so that religious instruction might be treated year by year with an increasing measure of neglect, the hour for giving it shortened,the syllabus, as it would be called, simplified. until it were reduced to mere reading of the Bible, the teachers being allowed to absent themselves, the chil- dren not pressed and never compelled to attend, then, he took it, as years went on, slowly, bit by bit, they 'blight get to be accustomed to a secular system and Secularism. As long as thsy had a denominational standard of religious instruction in their educational system that affected people's minds, it prevented religious instruction being neglected, and altogether forgotten. But banish that system from their educational machine, and then they did run a great risk. It was a very great matter in leed. Education meant the train- ing of those citizens who were in future to govern and rule over this country, of which they were all so proud. Let them not jeopardize so precious a heritage. Let them take care that those who came after them were filled with the spirit of right and wrong and the principle of faith in an unseen world, so they should indeed be worthy of what they bad to do. Let them then take care of their schools and guard them so that those who came after them might preserve and not lose the rich inheritance that was theirs. (Loud cheers.) The resolution was put to the meeting and declared carried by a large majority. A few persons held up their hands against it. The Rev. E. C. E. CARLETON moved :—" That we, in this meeting assembled, pledge ourselves to further in every lawful way the attainment of the foregoing reso- tution, and we authorize that the same be forwarded to the Prime Minister, the President of the Board of Education, the Member of Parliament for Warrington, arid Mr. A. J. Balfour for their support." (Applause.) He contended that it was ridiculous to make the Town Council a body having authority in matters of religion. Here it was that the Bill was ylainst the common law, as well as against common(justice and common sense. The common law had ever required spiritual qualifications in those who dealt authoritively with spiritual things ; that was a principle universally recognised, and upon which was founded that spiritual jurisdiction which from the first had been part of the common law of the country. This illiberal and religious Bill was lacking in the elements of common sense, of common justice, and he told Mr. Birrell, lawyer though he might be, that it was against the common law of the land, in which the liberties of our country wArs enshrined. (Applause.) Mr. JOHN WHITE seconded and the, resolution was carried by a large majority. The Bishop and Lord Hugh Cecil .s_were thanked on the proposition- of Sir GILBERT GREENALL, bart., seconded by Colonel BUCKTON. Similar resolutions to the above were adopted at the overflow meeting which WAS addressed by the Yen. Archdeacon Madtien.

WARRINGTON CHURGHPEOPLE'S

PROTEST. Visit of Lord Hugh Cecil.

, Warrington C,hurchpeople assembled in strong force at a meeting held in the Pair Hall on Frida evening to protest against the Education Bill. The visit of Lord Hugh Cecil had been looked forward to with great interest, and the hall was crowded in every part, an overflow meeting being held on the open space near Holy Trinity Schools. Before the meeting in the Hall began the audience joined vary heartily in singtpg well- known Church hymns, Mr. F. NI. Darby. Mug, an. pre- siding at the organ. On entering to take the chair the Bishop of Liverpool (Right Rev. Dr. Chavasse) bad a hearty welcome. He was supported by Lord Hugh Cecil, most of the local clergy and many leading lay- men. After the singing of "The Orturch's one Foundation" prayer was offered by the Rector. • The BISHOP said the Education Bill was read for the second time the previous night, and by a majority of more than 200. That fact need not perturb them. (Applause.) Their memories were long enough to remember that four years ago another Education Bill was read for the recond time, with a still larger majority. If it happened that this Education Bill became an Education Act, they mighil look forward 1 hopefully to the time when another majority equally barT ~.„, woold amend or remove it from the statute •ecut'r• (Anplause.) Meanwhile their policy was th ' TheY could do their utmost, to amend e Bill as it passed through committee. They could make the House of Lords feel that a very large number indeed of English people were whole- heartedly against the Bill and if it passed both Houses they could do their utmost to bring about its renell..l (Applause.) Their present duty, he took it, was to help ' the Government as far as thev.could to among the Bill on right lines. There Were certainly two good points about ' this Bill. First, it recognized religious education and secondly in its 4th clause it recognisad denominational . education. They should first aim to secure religion teach- ing for their children. (Applause.)Tlais Bill permitted it I but did not eecure it. It might. as a leading Church- ! man of the diocese had said, be celled a Bill to diseouvale religious teaching in the public elementary schools. It gave the local education authority the power to for- bid religious teaching, and it gave the teachers fall !: power to decline to give any religions teaching what- -ever. He was afraid that if that was the case, in small - country schools like Padgate and Hollinfa re, any disin- ,, elination of the head teacher might lead to giving no : religious teaching at all. Last hut not least parents ra-,„ght or might not send their children to be taught the ' °lble, catechism or Prayer Book, for religious teaching, according to the Bill, was to be outside school hours. !t Was for them to do their utmost to hale the word ' Bay" in regard to religious teaching deleted and the Word" Imust " inserted—(applsuse)—so that every local authority was bound to teach the children the rudiments Of the faith, that every teacher who could not con- scientiously give that teaching himself, might yet make - arrangements for it to he given and that every child at least might be within'the school walls when the religions teaching was given. (Applause. )Secondly,they must claim that definite Church teaching be given to every Church child— i (applause)—not only n what were called non- Provided schools—that was voluntary schools—but also ' ....in the provided and the Council schools. (Applause) varents had an inherent right to demand tbatthair chil- Idren should be trained in their own religions faith. A Parent was responsible for the spiritual training of his Child, and if the State compelled him to send his child , to school, the State was bound to provide that child With that kind of religious teaching which the parent I required. Chnrchpeople paid the grea+er part of the 1 rates and taxes, and the great constitutional principle Was that those who paid rates and taxes should have a , voice in their expenditure—(upplause)—and they had a right to ask that the money spant OD education should b. ^ !Pent on the lines that they liked, without infringing on the religious liberty of those who differed from _them. (Applause.) Why drew the line at at magical four-fifths? He could not for cue moment have—the Government bad certainly repudiated it— they deliberately introduced that principle to injure Church of England. THE ROMANCE OF PAPER MAKING,

W. T. PALMER.

(in the manufacture of paper loss Lingeo toe whole progress of civilisation. Nations have ever graded in point of culture mid power by their ability to produce and use this staple. The clumsy enevrolled brick of the As- syrian races, the costly and scarce parchments of the Persians, the cumbrous waxed tablets of ancient Greece, each carried these nations to the front of their more savage compeers, but their advancement is halting when compared with the intellectual progress of the Egyptians, whose everyday medium for the record and interchange of thought was the easily-prepared and con- venient papyrus. The subjects on which suc- cessive talented generations by the Nile ex- hausted their pcwers were by them sidvanced to a marked degree. The priests and literati, even the educated classes below them, of that far-off time possessed rich stores of various knowledge which modern research, from the vague border- land to which it has so difficultly attained, can do little more than guess at. After the fall of Egypt, and the centuries of riot of Barbarism in all parts of the world (save perhaps China), men again began to be dissatisfied that the no- blest acts of their times should be relegated to the uncertainties of legend and ballad, there- fore a new race of scribes began to live in the monasteries and courts of Europe. And per- haps previous to this desire for 'written histories, the copying of Holy Writ had been carried on. The merging of minor chieftainships into the grand domains and concerns of the Emperors also had its influence on the increased necessity for writing, and as parchment was so scarce the need for a new medium began to be more felt. Thus paper began to be prepared from pulped rags, from straw, from the barks of various trees, so that when the science of printing was again evolved (the Egyptians possessed this secret in its entirety ages before), a plentiful supply was available for the hand presses of Caxton, Wynkin de Worde, and their succes- sors. The history of paper, and the record of its various influences on nations and individuals, from this point onward to the present day, is too wide a subject for this sketch, so again long ages are stepped across to reach the modern story of how paper is made.

PAYER OLD AND NEW. Speaking commercially, paper is no longer made of rags. Within the lust thirty years four different staples have bee* used. The first was rags, then surat, a kind of bark brought from India; waste jute was next in use, and now wood pulp. The production of paper in these days is also a very different thing to that detailed by Herodotus in his description of the Deltaic, wonders. The learned Greek tells how the men of the Nile swiunps split the papyrus reeds and took from within the tough covering skin after skin of the most durable mat9.rial. Machinery costing many thousands of pounds is found in every modern paper mill; it is in charge of skilled men, its functions are governed by expert mechanics, the material is in charge of capable chemists. Of course, that luxury of connoisseurs, hand-made paper, is still produced in antique implements and. by time-honoured Methods. _

Among the trees in a Norwegian dell men with axes in hand are moving : one stops in front of this foot-thick sapling, and a few powerful swinging strokes bring it down. The branches are rapidly lopped away, and the trunk dragged to the water's edge. A push and away the log is whirled seaward. At the mouth of the river the timber is diverted into a cross-channel, floated to the wharf, and placed on board the vessel for export. Many of the logs from Norwegian forests, however, and all those set aside in Canada for paper manufacture, are reduced to pulp at factories by the shores of those countries. After ' passing the North Bea the lumber boat arrives at some English port., and here -its cargo, unloaded by steam cranes. is placed on railway trucks en route for the mills. The logs are thrown from the trucks into shallow ponds till required for use—a very nec- essary provision against fire.

THE FIRST PROCESSES, Now -the appointed hour has arrived, and a oatch of baulks are in the grappling iron. They arc carried into the mills and by various machines sawn into small pieces. These are placed in huge boilers with quantities of sulphur and other solvents, and are kept at a high bent for many hours. The wonderful structure of cells and fibres has by that time been broken down, and the resultant mass after being ground into fine fragments is drained and pressed be- tween damp rollers. The pulp, as it comes through this process, is formed into long sheets of the appearance of wet, neutral-tinted flannel. It is also soft and yielding ; a finger can be easily pushed through though the sheet is half an inch thick. Thus, in modem mills, the log is reduced to pulp in three or four days ; for- merly when water was the only known solvent the same took months to perform. Here let it be added that a portion of the world's wood pulp is not made into paper— papier mache, though in reality a distinct ma- terial, is often counterfeited by it, and . most of our cards are pulped sheets pressed tough and thin, and covered with a glaze, which permits them to be written upon.

mon PULP TO PAPER.

The nub to be converted into

ground-up—for hours this time With a lage quantity of water intermixed. The current of pulp and water is forced round and round an elliptical tank, passing into the " beater "-- shaped like the paddle-box of an antiquated steamer—on each journey. After hundreds of such passages the breaker fails to make any fur- ther impression on the particles which are then pumped away to another machine where a con- stant stream of water clears away all impuri- ties, chance lumps, &c. If the paper is to be col- oured, a quantity of strong aniline dye is mixed with it at this stage. Now, in turgid streams. the paper runs down towards the "making machine." This is a wonderful invention, at long succession, yet in perfect order, of troughs and pulleys and cogs, of felts and diverse sized wheels, of cold cylinders and steam-heated ones. The material as it is carried along completely changes its nature. Stand a moment by the machine, and consider its working. Note also the watchfulness of the man in charge. He is ever tearing out small squares which he weighs in a delicate balance ; his eye is upon the colour- ing ; upon the volumn which the machine is dealing with. Should any one of the various pipes fail in its supply the paper is spoiled in comparison to the sample lying before the man in charge. Then, too, the hundred-foot-long monster needs perpetual attention, for unless it runs smoothly the product is simply waste. The water with paper particles in solution runs down into a transverse tank from which it emerges in a broad current on to a finely wove endless wire mat, which is moving forward. So fine are the meshes that a hundred and sixty are found in a single square inch—the effect of these narrow interstices is that as the wire runs on its forty feet level, the water ebbs away. The little dots of material are left on the surface, and by their own weight fall into a soft pulp. The fibres with which each mite is clothed knit them- selves together, and the pressure of a flannel- covered roller completes the process of consoli- dation. After passing this roller the fabric is strong enough to hold together : it is carefully fed along felt blankets revolving at a slow speed, and then on to a cylinder, twelve feet in dia- meter, revolving, ,and heated by steam. This dries the paper, which is to the average eye al- most a finished product. Closer observation, however, shows that it could not be written or printed upon : the surface is rough and spongy, blurring at the touch of ink.

FINAL STAGES. The completing processes are now taken in hand A liquid size "of resin, china clay, and other nigieuienis o prepareu, mid the sees of paper unwound to pass through it. If one side only is to be " finished " this is com- paratively simple, but or, both sides to be treated the paper is conducted over a maze of rollers till it becomes difficult for the onlooker to say where the real course of the web lies. After sizing the paper is passed between hot rollers— steel tubes heated with steam —and comes out smoothed and polished—fit for the pen or the press. The hot-roller treatment is used to all classes of paper, for the pressure is sufficient to make unsized paper retain ink without blurring. The machine by which a reel of laiper six feet aide, and perhaps three quarters of a mile lcsig is cut into sheets, say 20 inches by 30 icates in size, is worth watching. Along a bar at the top of the frame four circular .knives are set twenty inches apart ; in front of them is long blade is fixed on a shaft miming from side to side of the maciane. when me paper is red in and the machine started, the circular blades divide it ac- curately, while the long blade revolves at a regulated speed so that when thirty inches of the reel have run it has journeyed round to the cutting point again. The deliberate motion of this knife when cutting a sixty inch sheet is in amusing contrast to the whirlwind of slashes when pieces of about ten inches long are going through. The divided sheets are individually examined: in some the colour is not quite even, a small crease snakes another considered imperfect. These sheets are thrown aside, and are sent back to a machine to be ground up, and afterwards re-made. The perfect sheets are counted hits reams, packed and marketed.

VARIOUS PAPERS.

Perhaps a line on two special varieties of paper may be of interest. That tough /awe] with a muslin fabric apparently interwoven, used for registered envelopes in our post-offices, is made by pressing the muslin into the half-made paper as it passes round the huge steam chest after the journey along the draining wire. What is called art paper is much used for some purposes— possesaing sidP.s of varying colour, say red and yellow, green and white. Most of this is made by varnishing a yellow sheet red on one side—an easy matter with proper machin- ery, but the first duplex papers were made by imposing two distinct sheets on top of one an- other in their half-made state, and pressing them together. Where does the immense production of paper go to. Counting-houses, packing-room, news- paper office, publishing house, each use their thousands of tons. Paper is 80 common with us that it is not valued, yet I see that tatter of newspaper fling along the street in the wind, and settling in some little dusty crevice of a wall—will that tmconsidered scrap ever be held of importance ? It may be so. The libraries of the ancients were destroyed. utterly, such may happen again in the case of our modern collec- tions of books, and the antiquarians of a thou- sand decades hence, may perhaps from fugitive fragments of newspapers and letters, be able to piece together our history, as our learned men have done in the case of the old Egyptian race.

SCIENCE NOTES AND NEWS.

SEA BOTTLES.

Curious experiments have been carried out by Captain Simpson of the steamship Moravian to test the course and spied of' ocean currents. For the past fifteen years, during the passage of this liner between Plymouth, South Africa, and Australia, the captain has every day thrown overboard a tightly-corked beer bottle containing a scroll bearing the latitude, longitude, and date when cast adrift, together with a request to the finder to forward the bottle to him at the Lon- don offices of the steamship line. The captain, who is a member of the British Meteorological Society, keeps a careful record of his daily opera- tions. The number of bottles returned to him, however, is very small, the average being less than one per year.. THE CAUSE OF APPENDICITIS.

Not a few medical men have favoured the theory that it is the introduction into the appen- dix through the food of some sharp irritating substance, such as pips, toothbrush bristles, grit in flour, particles of antimonial rubber stoppers, and so forth, that causes appendicitis. A lay correspondent of the Lancet makes yet another suggestion. "The present age," he writes, " uses increasingly enamel ware which splinters on the least provocation. I find that not only a food such U. porridge will carry the needle-like splinters to the body but a soup and even tea may very well contain sharply splintered particles of this most dangerous glass.' He concludes that we ought to return to the former plain iron and copper-ware utensils. There can be no ques- tion that the swallowing of spicules of enamel would involve some risk to health, remarks Science Siftings, but we are not aware that any cases of appendicitis have been conclusively traced to this cause.

THE SMALLEST MICROBES.

Dr. Staubli has discovered what he considers a hitherto undescribed bacillus, which is, he be- lieves, the smallest yet cultivated. He suggests that its discovery proves the possibility that vari- ous diseases which are now considered non- bacterial may be caused by similar organisms which have so far eluded observation by their minuteness and difficulty of cultivation. The patient ,was suffering from blood-poisoning. After about three weeks of illness he died. Blood cultures from his body shewed a tiny bacillus, growing on ordinary media at first only in the depths, afterwards also on the surface, but always only in small numbers: Its first appear- ance in the plates was much delayed. shewing tliat the plates of a Elood culture should always be watched during at least six days, even if one germ has already been identified. The doctor believes that the unknown germ was the cause of the infection,-

THE MAKING OF BUFFERS. ! Very few persons, apart from those whose busi- ness it is to notice machinery, seem to bestow any attention on the comparatively small but im- portant projections called buffers, on railway and other carria.zes, which add so largely to the cm- fort of the traveller, says the Magazine of Corn. t tnerce. It may casually be noticed that one type of buffer may be long, another short, but the passenger enters the carriage without thinking that but for the little appliance at the ends, and Possibly underneath, he would be shaken almost to pieces long before his .destination was reached. The buffer is continually acting; quietly and steadily the piston, with its large head, is being pushed in and out, and it is only when it is sent in so rapidly as to reach the bottom, and cause a nasty jar, or bump. that the motion is felt and we become aware of its existence. This, aid to our comfort, and the protection of the vehicle and our persona, is I a most interesting and intri- cate piece of mechanism within and without, and on it are expended all the science and skill which careful research can suggest. It has to be con- sidered that the finished buffer will be struck all day long, often with terrific force, and with all kinds of blows, mostly short and sharp—of alt the most wearing—and, therefore, the selected is a mild steel of the very best quality not too soft, lest a sudden blow should cause it .0 bend, nor too hard lest it should crack or t-Tznik —and either cast or forged into the required form.

THE RUSTING OF IRON. An important advance in the elucidation of this much-debated problem has been made by Dr. Moody, whose experiments are described in the "Transactions of the Chemical Society." This investigator has devised an apparatus in which little polished rods of soft Swedish iron containing 99-8 per cent of the metal can be brought into contact with water and air com- pletely freed from carbon dioxide. When the ends of the rods immersed in pure distilled water rested directly on the bent tube containing them, little discoloration occurred at the point of contact, but when the enas were surrounded with little knobs of paraffin wax so that the rods were nowhero in contact with the glass, then no rusting was observed even after bubbling through the apparatus more than thirty times the amount of air required to oxidise the metal completely to ferric oxide. On the other hand, when ordinary air containing carbon dioxide was introduced, the metallic surface rapidly became tarnished and much rust speedily separated. Moreover, it was shewn that, although the rust- ing of iron is effected by the ordinary commer- cial hydrogen peroxide solution, which is always acid, vet this oxidation is not produced by the redistilled peroxide, which slowly evolves half its oxygen at the surface of the iron without tarnishing the metal in the slightest. As a practical outcome of these experiments the rust from the interior of iron flushing tanks was ex- amined, sine in these vessels the metal is alter- nately and in rapid succession exposed to air and water. The rust was found to contain from 8 to 12 per cent. of its iron as fermis carbonate, the remainder of the product consisting of ferrous and ferric oxides. Here, again, the facts point unquestionably to the interaction of the metal and carbonic acid as being the initial „use of rusting.

WEATHER FORECASTS. Among the many subjects engaging the atten- tion of astronomers and meteorologists at the present time, none is of greater practical import- ance than that which aims at the discovery of a means of prrdicting the character of our t:er.Krus by observations of the sun, or at extending tio reriod over which weather forecasts can be made. Professor R. A. Gregory, writing in the Sunday at Home, says it is probable—indeed, it. is almost certain--that the prediction of weather over much longer periods than are now practicable. will be successfully accomplished when we possess a more intimate and continuous knowledge of the meteorology of the sun than we do at present. In the prediction of good and bad seasons, meteorologists are to-day little in advance of the priests and astronomers of ancient Egypt or Assyria; for the only forecasts that can be mad 3 are based upon the recurrence of cycles of changes. Unfortunately, this is true to a great extent because, although the knowledge of perio- dic changes in .the earth's meteorology over a cycle of- years is of a more exact nature than for- merly, the fundamental causes of the variations from year to year have yet to be found. Amient astronomers were able to predict eclipses with fair accuracy, because

===== 0002601_19060519_art0035.txt | included=true | included =====
THEY KNEW THE ECLIPRE CYCLE,

though they did not understand the true relation- ships existing between the sun, moon, and 'earth as members of tho solar system, or the law of gravitation. The discovery of this law was the key to all cycles caused by the movement of members of our system, and in the light of it predictions of eclipses are now made. On the other hand, no master-key has been found which will enable the cycles extricated from meteoro- logical records to be seen as oonsequences of natural laws. The sun is responsible for the great moveruenta of the atmosphere which circulates around the solid earth; and certain portions of the globe shew meteorological variations which coincide with variations of solar phenomena, year by year. But it is by no means possible to say, with truth, that a certain kind of weather is produced in the British Isles or any other part of the earth whet, certain phenomena are observed on the sun. It seems that we are to MR. BIRRELL'S receive from Mr. AUGUSTINS LATEST WORK. 13IRRELL, now that his opportunity has come, some exceedingly practical lessons in statesmanship. His Education Bill is a measure that in the present condition of feeling on the subject can scarcely be mended. It is, no doubt, creating a great storm, and is pretty certain to lead the House of Lords into acts of •extravagance, but this is because of the nature of the problem, and not of any inepiti- tude in Mr. 13fltantes propo-ed solution of it. To most men of Cabinet rank, even in so deter- mined a Government as the present, the piloting of such a Bill through Parliament would seem to be quite sufficient work for the time being, but I experts on the education question discovered on Tuesday that Mr. Binum has another important task in hand. He has produced an "Education , (Consolidation) Bill." We have no hesitation in say- ing that of its kind it is the finest and most useful measure ever introduced to the House of Commons. There is no difficulty in imagining how it has come into being. When he went to the ilw.rd of Education Mr. BIRRELL found that he had at least twenty Acts of Parliament to deal with, and that he could not advance a step without consulting one or other or the whole of them. These various Education Acts have been the standing puzzle of all persons interested in education. They have bewildered even the lawyers. The educationa system of the country was embodied, not in two or three Acts of Parliament, but in twenty, and it was not possible to interpret the system from a single Act, nor was it safe to attempt an interpretation which was not founded on a knowledge of the entire series. Mr. Binanu. evidently found himself entangled in a barbed wire structure when he wont to White- hall, and he at once, as it now appears, set him self to cut his way out. In doing so he has made a path for all other men, not only, that is to say, for his succeasors, but for all those who have any interest of any kind whatever in educational law. The Education (Consolidation) Bill is a splendid achievement. It proposes to put fifteen Acts of Parliament out of existence altogether by a pro- cess as summary as that of hanging. Four other Acts survive in some of their sections. One, the Act of 1902, is preserved, but in a severely lopPed

not to say mutilated condition. And what does Mr. BIELRELL substitute for all this confused lumber of law, this educational top hamper, as it may be called. He substitutes one thorougly plain, straightforward, quite intelligible piece of law- making, the most clear, direct, and businesslike thing of its kind that has ever been designed. Mr. I3ra.nELL is himself a llwyer, but he certainly can- not be described as the friend of lawyers. When his measure becomes law it will probably mean a loss of some hundreds of thousands a year to the legal profession. In place of twenty Acts of Parliament he' gives us what is really a lucid unpre- tentious text-book of education law. Everything is clearly sot out and defined in language concerning which there can be no dispute. Every subject is un- der its own head and is easy of reference. The char- acter of local education authorities and their

powers and duties come first in what may be called the opening chapter. In Part 11. of the Bill all that bears upon elementary education is explained in easily-understood language. Then comes the subject of school attendance, including the duty of the parent, the employment of children, school attendance orders, and what not. In less than seventy foolscap pages the entire law

of the country bearing upon education is made intelligible—if not to the meanest capacity,at least to the average mind—and this, it should be said, is an Educationßill that will be passed without serious trouble or opposition. It is a consolidating Bill, and nothing more. There is nothing of a party character in it. The measure contains nothing that can be complained of by any except those who have been accustomed to make a profit out of con- fusion. Should Mr. BIRRELL'S example be followed in other Departments of the State, there will shortly be more waste paper of the legal variety than can be easily dealt with by the exist- ing refuse destructors, but the statrites of the Itaalm will from henceforth be something that the plain man can understand.

We hope the townspeople PHOTOGRAPHIC ART. of Warrington will not be backward in taking the Opportunity of seeing the splendid photographic exhibition now being held in the local art gallery under the auspices of the 'Warrington Photo- graphic Society. If they are not greatly interested in camera work we would draw their respectful attention to the fact that the exhibition is free. On second thoughts they may recollect having heard that photography is an art with infinite interesting possibilities, and forthwith patronise the show. We do not think they will any of them be disappointed, unless they expect some- thing like a cinematograph, or the snap- shot of the latest execution. The visitor Cannot fail to realise that picture-making is not confined to the knight of palette and brush. Most of the photographs on view look as if they had never been acquainted with anything so mechanical as a camera—such advance has the art of taking photographs made since the ' day when the operator " snapped " the family tabby, and produced painfully true portraits of his relatives symmetrically assembled in the back- yard. The beauties of nature are, nowadays, almost excelled by the photographic expert, and Portraiture is a fine art wonderfully applied. It is a pleasure to see what excellent work is pro duced by local amateur photographers, whose studies hang worthily alongside what are recog bised as the country's masterpieces, and altogether 1 the exhibition is a credit to the Society and the

There were some lively pro- THE Foomut ceedings at the annual CLUB. meeting of the members of the Warrington Football 0' last (Thursday) night, and the retiring officers -atee had to stand the fire of keen, 1-natured criticism. A year ago all just quietness, but then the Club bad of the Noi 'brilliant season with the capture ; t. resolved Cup. Last night's mPeting _ inquiry relative to the series of failures -‘ made the past season the antithesis of the 'I one. The battle largely centred round tt, .lure in the first round of the Cup Competition and the Committee 7, had to admit that in the selection of the team I on this occasion an error of judgment was com- mitted. Little was said on the financial aspect of affairs, it being generally realised that the loss of £230 on the year's working was only moderate considering the poor season from a playing sense end the large decrease in revenue. At one period during the proccedings it looked as if there would be a clean sweep of the old committee, but luckily wiser counsels prevailed and there were only two changes. We have much pleasure in con- gratulating Mr. J. E. Warren on his . unanimous election to the presidency. He has a reputation as an administrator in Northern Union affairs,and we have no doubt that under his guidance the War- ington club will prosper. It is interesting to note that Mr. Warren shares the general pessimism as to the future of the Northern Union, and he boldly outlined the basis on which he hoped and believed a rapprochement with the Rugby Union might eventually be possible.

No doubt it is great fun for young people to indulge in horse play in Sankey-street on Sunday night. The pastime is certainly popular with the "young bloods " of both sexes and it seems rather bard for the law to step in and deprive "our boys "—and girls too—of such exhilarating pleasures. The peaceable and long-suffering citizen is, however, lifting up his voice in complaint—and rightly so. Matters have come to such a pass that the police have felt bound to take vigorous action and the magistrates are fully disposed to back them up. It quite time that a stop was put to the nuisance. Such novel recreations as pulling girls' arms and dresses and trying to trip them up, throwing lighted matches at them and 80 forth laay be harmless enough in a field,but in the public Iltreet they do not tend to the comfort of the more toberly-minded pedestrians. It is probably the tact that most large towns where there is a a.obatic promenade witness equally exciting scenes, but we want Warrington to set an example in deportment. Thoughtless youths had better realize that it is not their special privilege to create pandemonium in our main streets and annoy everyone around them. We are afraid that the girls are quite as much to blame as tho boys arid the hint from the Chief Constable that in fliture offenders of both sexes will be haled before the Justices will perhaps have a restraining effect.

NOTES AND NOTIONS.

Over 5,000 persons have been cremated since this form of disposing of the dead was introduced into the United Kingdom, says the report of the Cremation Society of England for 1905 We are to have a fancy dress parade in July in aid of the Infirmary extension. Such displays are par- ticularly popular with Warringtonians and the venture will no doubt be a I.l.iii,ge success. The " Tatler " has another good story this week. A fond parent says to his small son: "Wouldn't you like to see your little sister that the stork brought last night?" The young hopeful replies that "he'd rather see the stork"!

* * * , It is a significant fact that a gentleman in Sankey- street has placed a lifebuoy in a prominent position 'on his premises. Can it be that the deep pools in that street render such a precaution necessary for the safeguarding of the public? * * * John Stockton, Warrington's hero, has been granted the Stanhope Medal, the highest award for life-saving, as a sequel to his gallant adventure in Oe sewer fatality on March 6th last year. The Royal Humane Society has decided that Stockton's was the most I daring rescue of the year. He is the first Lancastrian to receive the Stanhope medal since 1873. All honour 'to him! * * * The candidate who advised his audience not to be frightened by the Home Rule red herring, because its bark was worse than its bite, has met his match. A Liverpool paper, discussing in a leading article the question of the city-bred populations, shrewdly observes that " many people are so essentially town- birds that they would be fishes out of water in the country." * * * Deputation work is often one of the most congenial duties which fall to public representatives and there was quite a lively competition at the meeting of the Board of Guardians on Wednesday for the honour of attending a London conference. Mr. George Mather, who was defeated by the Chairman's casting vote, is Iso anxious to accompany the deputation that he I announced his intention of doing so at his own expense. There is solid comfort in the words of Dr. T. C. Chamberlain, Professor of Geology at the University of Chicago. He announces that the world will be habitable for one hundred million years to come. But before settling down to enjoy its 100,000,000 years, humanity should satisfy itself that there is no close relationship between Dr. Chamberlain and another gentleman of the sonic name whose dealings with figures are famous in their way. Perhaps those hundred millions are merely "to illustrate the argu- ment."

* * * There was a record attendance of members of the Warrington F. C. at the annual nieeting in the Co-operative Hall last night, and :102 voting papers were handed in for the election of Committee. Four members of the retiring body were re-elected, viz., Messrs. Bowers, Locker, Hewitt and Davies, while F. Barber (the old County three-quarter) and W. Chalmers replaced T. Barnes and W. Nevins. The officials—. whose capacity is extolled year after year —were again elected without opposition. * * * The "Daily News" passes some strong strictures on Justices Grantham and Lawrance as a sequel to the abortive Yarmouth and Maidstone election petitions, and emphasizes the gravity of the position created. "At the last election," says the Journal, "the most extraordinary campaign of intimidation and bribery was carried on. At the next election , these practices will be increased a hundredfold, for Yarmouth and Maidstone have made it clear that the Corrupt Practices Act is only a scarecrow of which no candidate need stand in fear."

The cruel sentence pronounced by the Education Committee on Mr. Aylward, the Art Master, is down for rescinding at the meeting of the Committee on Monday next. Mr. Aylward is to he left to resign at with hth pi : bhis own convenience between now and July 31st, 9 which s u n arrangement, public andiopinion. . certainly W e wf feel o e elmorn believe, accord 1v hiswishesali pe or o and me in or keeping . e surprisedthat it was ever the intention of the Com- mittee to turn such an old and respected servant away at, practically, a moment's notice. * * * There have been some quaint clerical comments on the Education Bill, but the following from the Parish Magazine of Arlesey (lielts.) will ho difficult to beat. The Vicar writes : Let the Government only add secular educa- tion in the schools, then God help us all ! In two or three generations the Paganism that surrounded King Alfred will surround King Edward ! " Evidently the Vicar has no doubt about King Edward surpassing all his subjects in the matter of longevity !

* * * Even the Education Committee is learning, and my yet obtain the mark " excellent." We note that the School Management Committee has decided to make arrangements with the baths-keeper (subject to the consent of his employers) to give Instruction in swimming to the school children who attend the baths. Some day, we shall have swimming scheduled as one of the subjects for compulsory teaching to elementary scholars, and short of this the Education Committee have done tU, best thing possible. If Mr. Barlow can teach the children in squads —as they are taught gymnastics—well and good. Otherwise, he will require some assistance. In any case the results will be worth the cost. 55*

The discussion which took Ex-OFFicio place last week on the MAGISTRATES. clauses in the Justices of

the Peace Bill relating to the removal of ex-officio members of the magis- terial Bench whose conduct may have rendered that step necessary brought out very clearly the desire in all parts of the House for the mainten- ance of that high stand of public morals which is the proudest boast of English justice. Whatever the differences of opinion as to the provision in the Government Bill for dealing with cases such as must arise even in the best regula- lated communities, the aim was invariably the same. Mr. Rutherford pointed out that the , clause itself was inspired by the desire to avoid in I the future incidents similar to one he mentioned in which a Mayor, whose conduct had been such as to render his continuance on the Bench a public scandal, persisted in discharging the functions of a magistrate. That the Bill will enable the Lord Chancellor to deal with such cases is satisfactory to all parties ; and the assur- ances given by Sir William Robson that the law which applies to the removal of other justices will apply to ex-officio magistrates ought to pacify objectors. The point in which the public mainly will be interested is that the purity of the administration of the Bench in the lower courts will receive an additional security. England has, fortunately, had comparatively little to complain of in this matter,considering the number of magis- trates appointed to sit on the county and borough Benches. The standard of public office is unques- tionably high ; and the confidence which is felt in the administration of justice will be strengthened 'by a reform which happily called forth by a singularly low percentage of cases, provides an additional safeguard which must be welcome.

RUNCORN AND WIDNES.

SHOCKING FATALITY AT WIDNES.

Thomas Burns, aged twenty-four years, lodging in St. Michael's-road, Ditton, with a brother-in-law, hi. home being at 67, Anderson-street, Liverpool, met his death while following his employment as a labourer at Messrs. T. Davies's works, Ditton-road, Widnes, shortly after two on Tuesday afternoon. He fell from a ladder only a short distance from the ground, but received such severe injuries to his neck that he died soon afterwards. The body was conveyed to the hospital.

FATAL FLANNELETTE. William Woods, aged two, son of Charles Woods, engine driver, 5, Reay-street, Halton View, Widnes, died in the Accident Hospital at 7.40 on Saturday even- ing. About five o'clock on Saturday his mother put him to bed whilst her husband and herself went to Liverpool. They returned at 8.40. Alice, a twelve- year-old daughter,and a girl named Burgess were left in charge, and at 6.30 deceased walked downstairs. He was put back to bed, and went to sleep. Shortly afterwards Alice heard screams, and upon going to the bedroom she found his flannelette nightdress in flames. She pulled the gown from him, and burnt her hands in doing so, and afterwards went for assistance. Several neigh- bours applied oil to the boy's severe burns. Dr. Brobner was sent for, and ordered prompt removal to the hospital, where the boy died as stated. It is supposed that the poor little fellow got out of the bed, and was playing with matches, one of which became ignited, and set the fatal flannelette on fire.

ALLEGED FALSE PRETENCES AT WIDNES. A well-dressed young man, named Walter Jamesl Yandell, of 83, Jubilee Drive, Liverpool, was brought up at Widnes Police Court last Thursday, on a warrant eharged with having obtained £1 by false pretences from Samuel Hatton, of Witt-road.—Prosecutor stated that on the Thursday before Easter, he had an interview with the prisoner, who asked him to takeover an agency for the Falwell Cycle Company, and he agreed to do so. The prisoner said he was a bit hard up, and he gave him 10s. The prisoner promised to send him on two cycles , from Liv)rpool. He came again to see witness on the Saturday, and said he had sent the cycles from Liver- pool to Widnes Station. He also said he had forwarded ' £2 to the company on witness' behalf, and asked him to pay him that amount. Witness said he could only give him £l, and did so. He had not yet received the cycle.— The Superintendent said prisoner had only been arrested in the preceding night and accordingly applied for is remand.—Prisoner, who denied guilt, objected to a remand.—lt was, he said, entirely a matter between prosecutor and himself, and Hatton could give his evidence and he would give his, after which the Bench could deal with the matter.—Detective-sergeant Davidson said he apprehended prisoner at his residence at 6.30 p.m. on the previous day. When the warrant was read over to him prisoner admitted that he had received a sovereign from Hatton, but denied that he had obtained it by false pretences. When searched, prisoner was found in possession of a letter from the Rudge-Whitworth Cycle .Co.—The Superin- tendent said he wanted time to make inquiries regarding that letter.—Replying to the Mayo!, the. officer said prisoner had been living at Jubilee-drive for three weeks. He was a single man.—Prisoner was remanded for a week, bail being allowed in his own reiognisances of £5 and another surety of £lO or two of £5. RUNCORN BOARD OF GUARDIANS. The fortnightly meeting of the Runcorn Board of Guardians was held at Dutton on Tuesday when there were present : Mr. A. R. Norman (presiding), Admiral Jones-Parry, Messrs. S. Woodward, J. Spruce, H. Eyes, T. Wood, J. Hallows, T. Wright, T. Baxter, G. Egerton- Warburton,C. E. Linaker,C. E. Linaker,jun., J. Whitley, T. Guest, R. Billington, W. M. Morris, J. Douglas, P. Mounfield, J. Smith, T. Antwiss;J. H. Davies,G. Pilling, J. Fryar, E. Bretherton, W. Turner, H. M. Davies, W. Harrison, S. Horton, J. Wnitlow, G. F. A;-hton (clink), W. E. Hough (assistant clerk), J. Cunr-th (workhouse master), and Pearson and Cook (relieving officers). COMPLAINT FROM MR. WOODWARD. Mr. Woodward said he had received a complaint with regard to Mr. Pearson. A man in his (Mr. Woodward's) parish recently made an application for an order exempt- mg his child from vaccination, and produced a medical certificate to show that the child was not in a fit state of health to be vaccidated. The application was refused. Since then Mr. Pearson had visited the place, and in the meantime the man had been advised by the magistrates to take certain steps. Mr. Pearson when informed of this said the magistrates did not know any- thing about the law, and threatened to send Dr. Burton to see the child if it was not vaccinated. He thought that in making that threat Mr. Pearson had exceeded his duty. Mr. Pearson admitted in substance the accuracy of Mr. Woodward's lstatement. The child should have been vacinated three months ago but he knew that it was not fit to be vaccinated. He asked several times to be shown the medical certificate and being refused said he should send Dr. Burton to examine the child. It , was necessary that he should see a medical certificate Iso that he could place on record the reason for the child not being vaccinated. The Board thought that under the circumstances Mr. Pearson had not exceeded his duty and also that Mr. Woodward had been misinformed by the man who made the complaint. FINANCIAL. Mr. Woodward reported payments in out relief to Mr. Cook, £7O ; Mr. de Prez £26 ; and Mr. Pearson £25. Mr. Spruce reported that for the year ending March 31st there had been a decrease by 186 in the number of vagrants relieved as compared with 1905. The total number relieved was 2375.

Farrington (inspector) and Dr. Adams (medical officer). STOCKTON HEATH TREES. The Council were asked to confirm the minutes of a previous meeting granting the application of the Stockton Heath Parish Council to take over the trees in the village planted by the Tree Planting Committee. Mr. Linaker thought the Parish Council should also take over the responsibility of removing certain trees when called upon by the District Council. The High- ways Committee had not yet presented their report upon the trees but he understood that some were objected to. The clerk said that in his letter to the Parish Council he said: 'Of course if the District Council in the future require the removal ot any tree or trees their position will not be affected by the resolution." Mr. Linaker : That is perfectly satisfactory.
