EARLESTOWN AND NEWTON.

DRUNKENNESS AT NEWTON.

On Monday at the Newton Police Court, before Mr. F. J. Wood, John Murray, of Hand-and-Banner-yard, Newton-le-willows, was charged with having been drunk and disorderly on Saturday afternoon and on the evid- ence of Sergeant Bancks was fined ss. and costs. A MAN'S LEG CUT OFF.

A sad accident happened on Thursday last at the L. & N.-W. Goods Station, Earlestown. It appears two men were unloading bricks from wagons Ina siding for Mr. Lucas (contractor, of Prescot, for the erection of the new Wesleyan minister's house in Crow-lane East), when shunting operations were in progress. The wagons from which the bricks were being taken were suddenly shunted and Charles Critchley,of Prescot, who was in one wagon, was thrown over and fell between the buffers on to the line, where he was run over and his leg cut of. He was removed in the ambulance to Warrington. THE ST. HELENS CARNEGIE LIBRARIES.

At the meeting of the St.H elens Libraries Committee on Wednesday, the borough treasurer, Mr. Brown in submitting the estimate, said that, notwithstanding that the committee had two splendid new libraries to manage in addition to the three previously in existence, 'Ulu estimate of expenditure was the same as last year. It was estimated that £1,170 would be required for the Central Library, £2OO for Sutton, £lBO 'fbatto Heath, £l6O Parr, and £2OO Eccleston. From this total of £1,910 they estimated an income of £BO, leaving 21,560 to be raised by rates. Mr. Brown pointed out that after the cost of building the two new libraries was com- pletely made up, there would be about £lOO, which the committee would have to pay over and above the £6,000 given by Mr. Carnegie. The estimate was approved, ELEMENTARY COMMITTEE'S NOVEL SUGGESTION. Councillor Foote, chairman of the St, Helens School Atte.ndance Committee, on Wednesday reported to the committee that at thelmeeting of the Elementary School Committee earlier in the day it had been suggested that a nurse should be appointed to inspect children who were reported ill and unable to attend school, and that on her report the medical officer should be empowered ; to give a certificate instead of the parents having to go ; to their family doctor and pay for a certificate.—Mr. J. ! Phythian hailed this sugg,sstion with great satisfaction and said he had long regarded it as a great hardship on poor people that they should have to pay for certifi- cates, even when the child was only slightly ill. If the nurse was detailed to do this it would be one of the best things they had done.—The matter was left for the development of the idea by the Elementary Committee. , MISCONDUCT ON A TRAMCAR AT HAYDOCK. At Newton Petty Sessions last Thursday John Woodacre was summoned for disorderly conduct at Haydock, and assaulting Richard Wilde, tramcar , conductor, on April 2ith.—Wilde said the defendant I got on the car to ride only a few yards, and he advised him to walk as it would cost him a penny. He swore at him in reply and said he didn't care if it cost 2d., but after witness had punched his ticket he refused to pay. Wilde would not let him leave the car without paying, and a passenger who went to his help was seriously hurt by the defendant.—John Fletcher, the passenger named, said defendant knocked the conductor off the car. He (witness) helped to get defendant down on the ground, and when he got up again he kicked witness on the wrist so that he could not follaw his employment for two days —Defendant had nothing to say except that he was drunk at the time.—Fined 10s. and costs.

ALLEGED WOUNDING AT ST. HELENS. CHARGE AGAINST A WIFE.

Margaret Marsh, of 15, Barton-street, was at St Helens, on Monday charged with unlawfully wounding her husband. Henry Marsh on Saturday night.—Chief Inspector Goodall asked for a remand until next week on account of the man's condition.—Marsh said that he had been married to the prisoner about two years. They had both been married hefore. Mrs. Marsh had three children and the man had one boy, over whom frequent quarrels arose. About half past eleven o'clock on Saturday night, after a prolonged squabble, it was alleged that Mrs. Marsh picked a knife off the table and struck her husband ia the cheek, inflicting a wound three-quarters of an inch long. Police-constable Spencer took the couple to the Town Hall, where Mrs. Marsh was locked up. Marsh was attended by Dr: Reid, who said that owing to the filthy state the knife was in at the time he could not say how the case would go. The man's face was already much swollen.—Mrs. Marsh alleged great brutality on the part of her husband, who, she said, indulged in a prize-fight almost every week-end. The trouble did not %rise about his little boy.—The bench granted the woman bail. STREET BETTING AT BARLESTOWN.

At Newton Petty Sessions on Thursday,before Messrs. T. J. Gillespie (chairman), R. Stone, and F. J. Wood. Frank Dale, who did not appear, was summoned for street betting on three separate dates in Sankey-street, Earlestown.—P.S. Barrow was watching with other police officers, and saw. on Tuesday, April 24, six men and boys give slips of paper and money to Dale ; on Thursday, 26th, one man handed him a large number of slips with money, and Dale afterwards paid money out; on Wednesday, May 2nd, I the same thing occurred at several different times.— Fined £5 and costs in each of the three cases, defendant having been frequently summoned before.—Peter Singleton was summoned for similar offences in Viaduct- street on the 24th and 26th April. P.S. Barrow proved the case.—This defendant had not been convicted before and the Bench let him off with a fine of £2 and costs in each case.—James Foden was summoned for similar offences on May 3rd and 4th i in Wargrave Old-road.— Mr. Steel, defending, said there were two summonses, but there was a mistake in the date as to the second, and the Superintendent would withdraw that, defendant pleading guilty to the first charce.— P.C.'s Rimmer and Knott were watching near the Sun- beam Inn, and saw men go to the defendant at three different times of the day.—This being his first convic- tion, Foden was fined £2 and costs,—James Holmes, summoned for aiding and %betting Foden on May 3rd and 4th, was also defended by Mr. Steel.—P.C.'s Rimmer and Knott saw Holmes cross the road near the Sunbeam and give Foden slips and money.—Mr. Steel : How far were you away ?—P.C. Famine : About GO yards.—With a wall between you ?—Not exactly between us, and it is only 30 or 36 inches high.—He only went up to him once?—Only once on the 3rd.—The Superintendent: You.could see quite clearly ?—Yes. —Mr. Steel said in face of the evidence he should advise his client to plead guilty. He was a working man—not a bookmaker ; and he hoped it would be a warning to him not to do this again.—Fined £2 and costs.

NOREEN' WATER SIFPLY. The Clerk reported that the Local Government Board had written requesting to be informed of the result of the Council's consideration of their letter of March 9th respecting the question of water supply to 'Norley. Mr. Woodward said the committee which had been appointed to consider the question recommended the Council to approve the scheme for the supply of Norley from the Vyrnwy mains, but that for the present the Council do not go further with the work' than was contained in the smaller scheme submitted to and rejected by the residents of Norley. They recommended I also that a meeting of the committee and the Parish 1 Council he also held to see it a scheme could not be I agreed upon. An inquest was held on Tuesday afternoon by ' Coroner Brighouse on the body of Mary Worrell, a spinster, aged 79 years, who, for the last two months, bad been living with Mrs.. Braban, at 6, Poacher's- lane. Miss Worrall formerly lived alone at 48, Thelwall-lane, where she met with an accident on Sunday, February 18th. It appears that she was walking across the kitchen to get an apron out of the cupboard, when she fell. Dr. Braide was called in and found that she had fractured her arm. She went to live with • Mrs. Braban, and flied, at her house on Sunday morning. The jury returned a verdict of accidental death. SIN-GUINEA CYCLES. Excellent value. Call and I inspect.--J. S. Councillor Ball who presided, stated that life could only be noble and true if governed by the principles laid down by Jesus Christ. Men would fight, and even persecute others in order to maintain some creed or dogma, but it they wished to uplift and ble;ft their fellow men creed and dogma must go, and Jesus Christ must be exalted. The Bolton School.

The Sites and Buildings Committee of the Educa- tion Committee have made arrangements with Messrs. J. E. Wright, Garnett and Wright—" to obtain a tender for the erection of the Bolton 'Council school at a cost of not more than £lO 10s. per head in accordance with the decision of the Committee at their last meeting, assuming that " a reasonable margin" to that amount will be allowed." The Com- mittee is now inquiring what is meant by a "reason- able margin."

- LADIES' BEST GLACE BOOTS AND SHOES, UNDER THE CLOCK, BRIDGE-STREET.—Advt. Motor 'Bus Scheme.

On Tuesday- -evening the Mersey Electric Railway Bill was brought up in the House of Commons for second reading. One clause would enable the Com- pany. to run motor buses in competition with the municipality of Birkenhead The Bill was referred:to committee with an instruction that this clause should be deleted. The question is one which effects munici- pal ties very seriously and at the request of the War- rington Corporation Mr. A. H. Crosfield, M.P., voted against the Bill in its original form. LAwx MOWERS, Garden Tools, Sus. Splendid selection.---. S. GARLAND, 51, Bridge-st.—Advt.' Warrington Man's Bravery Rewarded. At the annual meeting of the loyal Humane Society in London on Tuesday the Stanhope Gold Medal for the most daring rescue of the past year was awarded to John Stockton, a baker's carter, of )Warrington for bravery which he displayed in connection with the gassing fatality at Howley early last year. It will be remembered that while engaged cleaning a sewer on I March 6th a workman was overcome by foul gas and became unconscious. 1 Two fellow-men went to his help, and were also struck down by the gas. Stockton volunteered to try to save them, and although he I knew nothing of sewer work he descended the manhole. IHe was at first driven back. A second attempt was more successful, and he brought up one man alive. A third and fourth time he went down, and brought lip the other two, but one of them was dead.—The Stanhope Veda' is the highest and most prized award given for', life-saving. This is the first occasion since its institution in 1873 that it has gone to Lancashire. .

WARRINGTON PHOTOGRAPHIC SOCIETY. Another Successful Exhibition.

The walls of the Municipal Art Gallery are this week hung with numerous choice specimens of the photo- graphic art collected for the Warrington Photographic Society's third open exhibition. Warrington has many enthusiaAic devotees of the -o:unera and the enterprise of those who take an interest in arranging these shows which have become so popular is to be commended. Not • only are the towns- people afforded the opportunity Which is I much appreciated of seeing a 'fine display of pictures of great variety, but local amateurs are able to compare their wotk with that of leading photo- graphers from other places and a worthy spirit of emulation is fostered. This year's show, which opened on Thursday afternoon and will continue until next Friday evening, is in every ,respect a credit ti the promoters. The members of the society are well to the fore with pictures of high merit and there has also been a gratifying response from outside exhibi- tr. The entries number nearly 500—an advance on last year—and we are pleased to note a great improve- ment in the method of hanging the pictures which are conveniently placed in consecutive order. Testimony to the high standard of the exhibition is forthcoming from the judges (Mr. A. Horsley Hinton and Dr. C. Thurston Holland, F.R.P.5.,) who have had a wide experience, and state that the show compares very favourably with anything they have seen. They also praise the manner in which the hanging arrange- ments have been carried out. The prizes consist .of tastefully-designed plaques and certiScates, for which competition has been keen. The work of some of the best- known photographers in the country is on view, many of the specimens having come from the Northern Exhibition at Manchester. THE EXHIBITS.

The antiquities room which leads to the art gallery is being used for the admirable loan collections placed at the disposal of the Society. The visitors' attention is first attracted on entering by hair a dozen pictures by Mr. Alvin Langdon Coburn,oue of America's foremost ' photographers, representative of his work in different branches of the art. A portrait of Mr. Horsley Hinton is particularly striking.. It is soft in outline, yet contrasts strongly with the dark hack ground and altogether is full of character. Mr. Coburn personally attended to the banging of his works and expressed his. pleasure at the general character of the exhibition. Then there are ten splendid pictures by Mr. Horsley Hinton, editor of" The amateur photo- grapher, "affording a capital idea of the features which he has so successfully specialised. Dr. C. Thurstan Holland, F.R.P.S., one of the leading photographers of tbe north, shows two fine glacier and snowscspe studies. and a very impressive view of an Alpine valley whose depths are in snaclow and whose heights are in the clouds. Passing to the art gallery.the member!' section is found to be a strong one.and will be of particular interest to local visitors. Mr.W.llPodinore carries off a plaque with . "The Water Carriers "—a study warm brown. Two country maidens are depicted getting water from the village spring in a picturesque nook. The figures are very naturally posed and contrast effectively with the surrounding rocks and shrubbery. Mr. •Kenneth F. Bishop's simple yet picturesque sandhill view " On the beach "—which won the Society's competition for members—likewise obtains a plaque. The same exhibitor' is certificated for a fine study " Tn pinewood," the tall tree trunks and the ever present gloom being admirably portrayed. Mr. Henry Bond is awarded a certificate for" Returning to the fold "—a pleasant summer evening view showing a flock of sheep returning fold-wards along a shady road by the lake-side. Mr. J. W. Mills is also successful with a striking pic- ture—" Surf "—in which the silvery moonlight stream- ing over the breaking surf produces a charming effect. Mr. A. C. Smithson's "In our alley,"which is certificated in this class and carries off a plaque in the members' section of those who hit've never previously taken an

award is a very creditable piece of work giving one a glimpse through a dark cobbled alley with high lights in the distance. The other plaque in this class goes to Mr. F. Garside who takes his first award with • A Roman match seller "—depicting h ragged old man sitting beneath au antique carved buttress. A print somewhat fuller in tone would, we think, have improved the effect. To deal with the winners of certificates, Mrs. A. C. Smithson shows a portrait of the oldest member of the Society (Mr. James Harding), the likeness being excel- lent and the toning and treatment very tasteful. Mr. J. B. Tolley is to the fore with a rugged, strongly-lit male head and Mr. J. Mawdsley shows a vievr bf "Hills above Nelson," the outline of the shoulder of the hill being broken by silver birches standing out against the sky. There is a good display of lantern slides. Plaques are awarded to Mr. R. F. Bishop who exhibits a splendid miscellaneous set and to Mr. F. Garside for his fine views of street scenes in Rome. The winners of certificates are Mr. F. V. L. Matthias and Mr. W. H. Podmore (miscellaneous collections.) The open classes are very representative and will repay careful attention. Five plaques are awarded. A well-known worker, Mr.DanlDunlop,has a study entitled" A fair Nor- wegian," a simply draped figure softly reproduced in a pleasing light brown tint. The satins exhibitor is also suc- cessful with a fine reproduction of Japanese anemones. "The river Medlock--an impression" by Mr. J. Dudley Johnson has attracted considerable attention. The photo- graph is taken from beneath a sombre archway and depicts the stream wending its way between towering warehouse walls. St. Sebastien "by Mr. S. Tries is a peculiar landscape effect something after the style of Maurice Griffenhagen, the black and white artist. " Winter " by Harold A. Blades is full of artistic merit and repre- sents a scene admirably suggestive of its title. The pictures carrying off the certificates are also very meritorious productions. Mr. Charles J. Harrison has a splendidly illuminated and crisp view of " Tho font, Tideswell. ' "Flying gannets" by Ur. William Farren is a fine snap-shot of the familiar sea bird sailing through the air on black-tipped, outstretched wings. Mr. Robert Burnie exhibits an attractive study of" White currants" with tthe berries almost trans- parently delicate. fa the pictorial photograph class for exhibitors who have never taken an award at an open show, two plaques were awarded. Mr. James Seddon is successful with "An idyll," dispicting a quiet stretch of river with the water receding from prosaic mud banks beautified by the reflection of subdued 'sunlight. Mr. Henry Holt is the other winner with a small hut meritorious head study "An old Volendam salt," With regard to the certificate awards, there is a beatitiful view of a sunlit corner in York Minster by the Rev. E. Charnock Smith, while " Sunlight to shadow" is a very clean, sharp print of a woodland glade by Mr. Wm. R. Irwin. "Cleaning nets" a typical seaside scene by Mr. S. Trias is an excellent print in a very poor frame. The lantern slide section is very well filled and plaques are awarded to W. E. Dean (scientific and archi- tectural sets), Robert Burnie (miscellaneous), and Alfred G. Thistleton (miscellaneous and architectural), and certificates to Graystone Bird (miscellaneous), W. H. Goy (flower studies), Dr. G. 11. Rodman and W. H. Tomkinson (miscellaneous). There is a great profu- sion of pictorial postcards and some particularly strik- ing examples of this branch of photography are shown. The successful competitors are :—E. Seymour (plaque) Rev. E. T. Clarke, William Baldwin, and J. R. Richardson (certificates). Thete are, of course, many pictures which did not catch the eye of the judges but which will probably appeal directly to the casual observer. Mrs. A. C. Sinithson's picture " Daddy" is an exceptionally creditable and well rendered composition. Mr. Henry Bond has a photograph of Harlech Castle standing out boldly from its commanding height, and Mr. D. S. Birrell's "On the lea" shows a full rigged barque sailing strikingly into the picture with the light,behind. "In the market place, Biskra," by the Bev. John Oldluim, is a pleasing study of white clad Arab youngsters gossiping ih the bright sunshine. Mr. Mr. E. G. Hackford has several very striking works. " Sunrise at Boston "is a fine " fazzYgraph" in gum which has attracted much attention. In another study Mr.Hackford portrays"Cupid"—less rotund than usual— busy sharpening his arraw. Perhaps the most noticeable portrait study is Mr. Hackford's Nearing the end," which shows a wonderfully seamed and wrinkled old dame who might readily pass for Methusulah's mother. Among many sandhill scenes one of the brightest is "The sand dunes" by W. H. Tomkinson. A small portraits somewhat out of the common is "The veil" by P. K. Smith. It is of the cabinet size toned pale green and artistically mounted. Special mention should be made of the natural history work of Mr. W. Farren of Cambridge who convincingly illustrates the valuable use the camera can be put to in aiding the study of this branch of research. Mr. James Hardiag, the oldest member of the Society, has several pictures (not for competition) including some splendid flower studies and a striking portrait "pleased to see you " which was appropriately placed at the entrance of the gallery. There is only one trade exhibit—a screen from Messrs. Griffin and Sons displaying an attractive portrait.

THE TEA ROOM. A new departure has been made this year by the transformation of a lecture room into a tea room which has been very well patron;zed. Pleasing decora- tions have been carried out by Mrs. Mathias with the able assistance of Nlrs.Smithson and Mrs.Smith.and the room presents a very cosy and inviting appearance. Mrs Mathias is also responsible for the very tasteful draping of the two exhibition galleries.

The general arrangements for the exhibition have been splendidly carried out, and a special word of praise is duo to Mr. A. C. Smithson (the secretary) for his invaluable organisation of the whole enterprise. THE OPENING CEREMONY. 'the opening ceremony was performed by Alderman Bennett, and was well attended. Mr. E. J. HALL, who introduced Mr. Bennett, said he must take the opportunity, standing as he did in the art gallery, to omy a word in, recognition of the fact that during the past few days one of Warrington's revered townsman, Mr. J. C. Thompson, had passed away. With the portrait of the deceased gentleman on the wall facing him he could recall very grateful memories of the days when, as one of Air. Thompson's pupils, he had the benefit of his wise teaching. Mr. Thompson's name would Always be revered and remembered in Warrington. One of their worthiest townsmen, Mr. Thompson had enshrined him- self in the memories and affection of his pupils. (Applause).

Alderman BENNETT said it was once again his pleasure and privilege to declare the exhibition open. The position in which he stood was certainly not due to any knowledge which he possessed of photography but was due entirely to the fact that he had the honour of being the chairman of the Museum Committee. He thought that the Museum was an institution so broad as to include within its scope everything which made for culture, beauty and art and all that was highestiand it was therefore not inappropriate that the Mu3eum,and its various associations should officially, through him, welcome the Warrington Photographic Society. In the tribute which Mr. Hall paid to the memory of Mr. Thompson they recognized one reason why in War- rington they ought to extend a sympathetic considera- tion to anything of an artistic character. Re always thought that amongst the various things which reflected credit upon Warrington certainly not the least were the associations which clustered round the name of Mr. J. C. Thompson and the eminent artists who received their first tuition from him in a room almost adjoining the one in which they were standing. (Hear, hear.) The Photographic Society was an institution which seemed to grow in popularity and prosperity year by year and although he had only had a cursory glance round he thought he was correct when he said that was the finest exhibition %hich the Society had ever held. They had rapidly outgrown the hospitality extended to them at the Old Academy and it looked ns though they would outgrow the hospitality of the Museum—a thing which he thought was another argument in favour of the extension Of the Museum, which he hoped would qot bkdeferred. The diffusing among the people ' of the long a love of beauty and'culture. must act in a most beneficial way upon the town, and he hoped the exhibition would be a reclrd both as regarded attend- ance and artistic merit. (Applause.) Mr. MAT ref As in moving a vote of thanks to Alder- man Bennett for his services, remarked that the Society had always received the greatest kindness at the hands ,of Alderman Bennett, both there and at the Old Academy, and he had done everything he could to help them in every possible way. Ile also desired to thank the Museum Committee and , the officials for the kindness and courtesy which they I had extended to the :Society. Since they commenced holding exhibitions on a larger scale the work of the Society had improved very much and members hnxi gone in for open competitions,and,he was glad to say,secured awards throughout the country. (Applanse).

Mr. ROBERT PimiporYr seconded. Fro had great sympathy with the Photographic Society, being himself a photographer of many years' standing, having begun in the days of wet plates and whorl they stained their hands with nitrate of silver. (Laughter.)Tt had been a great pleasure to'him now that under certain altered circuinstanues which did not necessitate his constant attendance at Westminster, and relieved him of responsibility,he had a littlecompensat- ing holiday time with his wife during which he had done a great deal of work with the camera. Alderman Bennett took a keen, nay almost a fevered, interest in matters concerning Warrington and especially concerning the old things and objects of beauty in Warrington which no doubt would be found represented among the photo- graphs in the exhibition. BEFORE THE MAGISTRATES.

SHOP-BREAKING AT WARRINGTON. At Warrington, on Saturday, Wm. Campbell, thgoe's-lane,Charl ea Woods,Ed win Woods,and William Woods—three brothers—Howard-street, were charged with breaking into the shop of Mr. Edwin Ambrose Jones, tailor ar d d raper,Priestley-street, and stea ling a quantity of drapery of the value of £l4 10s.—Superintendent Monathan said t're shop was locked up on Thursday night, and on Friday morning the premises were found to have been entered, and a quantity of drapery goods were missing. Constable Arms rong received informs: tion which resulted in the arrest of Charles and Edwin Woods, who afterwards made a statement implicating Campbell, who was arrested by Detective Clough. When being taken to the police station he became very violent, kicking the officer several times on the leg. The stolen goods had been left at the house of a Mrs. Dickenson, in Lythgoe's-lane, by Camp- bell, who led her to believe that he was removing. Mrs. Dickenson was charged at the police court on Wednes- day with receiving the goods, knowing tbem to have been stolen: but, considering the straightforward manner in which she had acted, he (the superintendent) would withdraw the case against her. He would also withdraw the case against Edwin Woods, the evidence showing that he had nothine to do with the robbery. The other threa prisoners pleaded guilty. Campbell, who has been convicted several times for theft, was committed to gaol for six months on the robbery charge and two months for the essaelt on Detective Clough. The brothers Charles and William Woods were sent to gaol for one month each, and Edwin Woods was dig- charged. ~ SUNDAY NIGHT PARA nERS.—DISORDERLY YOUTHS FINED. The disorderly conduct of young people who habitu- ally freqnent Sankey-street and the principal streets of the town on Sunday evenings has long formed the sub- ject of serious complaints to the police, and steps have been frequently taken to bring about a more orderly state of affairs. From time to time batches of youths have been fined at the Police Court for indulging, in horse-play—noth'ing more serious has ever been sugges- ted against them—to be followml in every case by a nariod of quiet. Gradually, however, the restraining influence of a saluta-y lesson thus afforded has worn I off and the police have been again compelled to bring the offenders before the local magistrates. On Monday five youths were summoned for disorderly con- duct in Sankey-streat and were each tined in the costs (4s. 6d.) They were Charles Austin, 12, School Brow ; Harry Hanson, 33, Willis-street 7 • Albert Wakefield, 63, Orford-street ; William Ford, 5, Lythgoes-lane, and Thomas Rowlands, 32, Lythgoes-lane.--In regard to the first two it was shown that they tried to trip up a girl, arra swung another girl round by the arms, sending her I from one t the other. The other three defendants, it was prosw., caught hold of girl's dresses and threw lighted matches at them.—The Cnief Constable said that on Sunday night Sankey-etreet and the immediate neighbourhood were scarcely fit for respectable women and girls to walk along owing to the conduct of otherwise highly respect- ahle yoeths—conduct which individually was not serious but which collectively formed an intolerable nuisance. On occasions when goine along Sankey-street he had been ashamed to be the chief of the police in the towa.— Mr. S. Wright, one of the magistrates, suggested that some of the girls who contributed to the disorder should be brought before the magi str ates.—The Chief Constable said that after that expression he would have no hesita- tion in bringing the girls into court. Previously he bad felt some little delicacy about doing so. THEFT OF SCRAP IRON. The New Schools.

BAZAAR TO CLEAR OFF THE DEBT.

A valuable work is being done by Ellesmere-strc3t Mission, Avhich is a centre of great activity in a working-class district. A. long-felt want was r ently supplied by the erection of commodious new school premiser., and it is the general opinion tbat the Sunday school with its 800 scholars is a crerit to the circuit. . debt still remains on the undertaking, and the workers are applying themselves diligently to raising the necessary funds. This week a bazaar, by which it is hoped to raise £2OO towards this laudable object, has been held, in Bold-street schools. It was opened on Wednesday afternoon, and w :11 close this (Friday) evening. A needy-compiled handbook contained an interesting history or the mission which dates • back to 1884. The ministers of the Circuit at the time were :—Revs. E. 0. Coleman, A. Agar Beet, and Ftancis Hagan. See dal services were being held in Bold-street Church and several youths became attached to the Church, some of whom are still workers in the Mission, and others who take a deep interest in its present work. To sustain the interest of these new members, a Mission Service was commenced in the Buttermarket-strr at schoolroom on Sunday evenings at 8 o'clock, after the usual preach- ing service at Bole-street Chapel. Thee inc ttings were carried on for some ti me under the leadership of Mr. J. B. hole, W. C. Nicholson, T. Prir ce, W. Charles,Joseph lithson and others. Eventually the Mission was trens- ferred to Hall-street, where a room was kindly lent by l•ir. J. Ernaress, and became know a as " The Coal Yard Mission." It was here that the workers commenced their first Sunday school under the superintendency of Mr. Gregson. For twelve months a good work ass carried on, and it was found necesrary to look for more commodious premises. In the year 1888, the upper story of the old building in Buttermarket-street was again occupied, and for a few years a steady progr, -a was noticeable. The number of scholars at this time was about 200. At the lathr end oa the year 1892, a communication was received from the trustees of the Sunday School, that the premises had deen dispond of, This was a sore disappointment to the workers of the Mission, for they felt that the work which had hr n conducted by the Mission so successfully for meny, would probably have to be relinquished. For three months the Mission had no place of meeting, and there was much anxiety concerning the scholars and membetJ of the congregation. As a result of a Conference in December 1892 various sites for a new building were inspected, and the only one suitable was in Ellesmere- street. The wisdom of the choice is undoubtedly shown in the progress that has been made. Four gentlemer (Messrs. J. B Isaac, J. Efartley, W. Brough and R. Dale) came forward, and kindly promised to find the means for erecting an iron Cbapel in Ellesmere-street. The erection of the building was immediately commenced, and on the 23rd April 1893, the opening services were held, Rev. J. Kirtlan conducted the service in the afterooon, and Mr. Brough in the evening. In November 1896 the debt on the building was paid, and when the wealth of the workers in the Mission is taken into consideration, every praise is due to them. Our workers are all workinc people, but the noble sactifices they have made for their church is woi thy of the highest commendation. The Mission since its inauguration has passed through many trying circum- stances, but the fidelity of the workers has remained immovable. The success achieved is attributed tto the kind and brotherly love that exists between the mem- bers. The workers are proud of the Mission, and of the 'Sunday school, and the latter is a credit to the Circuit. In the new premises there is better scone with the senior scholars, and the congregation are looking for- ward with hope for a continuance of the success of the 'Past. The Sunday school at present under the super- intendency of Mr. John Barlow, flourishes beyond the dreams of all connected with the institution of a few years back. There has been a succession of superin- tendents of well-known names in local Methodism : B. Isaac Joseph Dobagn,William Gregson,Levi Richard- sor George Perkins, W. H. Shertnp a, and John Barlow. tic: Mission organizations include a book club, penny bank, Band of Hope, three eociety chases, a Young :ruer's Union, and a cricket club. The following executive committee have bad the bazaar weangernents in hand :—Prrlident, Rev. J. Manning ; secretaries. Messrs. F. Cox, E. Horton ; tre Ismer, Mr. J. Taylor; Messrs. E. Howard, C. Dri water ; Misses Leatherbarrow. and C. Humphreys.

The hall was filled every Sunday ni eht and the Sunday school was the second largest in the circuit, there being over 400 scholars on the books. THE QUAKE, THE FIRE, AND STILL THE QUAKE.

Another Letter from Mr. H. Whitham.

I am afraid the Pacific coast of North America is not a desirable residential district, and I think it corld never at any time have been pronounced, with a due regard to truth, " perfect." I think it was within a very few weeks after our arrival here, some eiglween years ago, that we received during the night our first experience of a pretty good shock of earthquake, and to-day, just one week after the biggest quake the white man has known 'since he came to live here, we have had another shake, and it was one float has settled he question about quitting the city of San Francisco. Yes, we must r ye on, once more. Possibly, we may land in C a towa just across the bay, and although it is still within the earthquake zone, it re-ay be possible for us to get another house all to ourselves, fiats not being the only residential style of housing the meek and lowly. So it is that house-hunting has commenced once more, anclA am wondering who must do it. Not I, not being able to walk without a stick, id a limp, I guess I shall be told to " stay at home." When the quake of this afternoon can- e along I was with one of my married daughters who livci among the hills a mile away. It was nothing to 13 scoffed at. If it had been a lonely visitor, it vraAd have had a line in history all to itself. As it was I thought it wise to get off -home and see how things were there They were bad. My wife and two daughtera Ruby and flora, seem just now pretty nears a nervous wreck and would not on any account stay another night in the house. so mother and I stayed in the house alone. There is another daughter, Marian, I have not seen her for four days. She is a nurse at one of the wrecked hospitals, and I am hopirg she has stood the most recent quake better than her sisters. Now, who is to do the house hunting? I don't know but we shall and must have it done, and get out of this city of 400,000 inhabitants. The number would not b equif so h. Li as this,l think, if we had a count to-day' The rail ,ds say they have taken 25,000 out, and the steamboats have taken out a big 'number. But they will take no farnitnre out of the city. My next door neighbours on the ground floor, after two days shock,just pack: i their hand bags and shook the dust of the city their feet, and quit. It was quite easy fur a large number of these people to quit. An immense number of them lived-in furnished rooms, and the American family, as a rule, is not large. So we have Oakland as the objective point. So much for the domestic side of the results of Quake and fire r id still quake I can make no further at' rapt to describe the upheaval. In this hest of all possible worlds we found in a few short w ;on& er,. water, our gas, our electricity and all that electricity means suddenly taken from us. Something else:l may just whisper. We are quite sure we can Imini t‘,- all you who live outside our famous city and roe :e you believe there was no earthquake, and this although our press acknowledge 1,000 killed and I don't know how many injured. Our City Fathers have met and have solemnly resolved that the disaster shall be knowa as " The great fire of 1906, and not as the great earth_ quake." I see in the paper to-day that the Mayor, represent- ing the civil power, has given the military power a , little of his mind, and declares the city has not been under martial law. This is rather strange, inasmuch as it is quite certain a number of very respectable citizens end at least one woman have been shot dead, in sight. Several of these blood-thirsty mit. creants, who are first to fight and last to toil, are now held for murder. Give a majority of the.class who enlist, the right to carry out all that is meant by Martial Law, and we should soon see hwr deep our civilization is. Two soldiers, they seemed two decent fellows have just been to my door, and said that if we wished we could have a special guard, as the Thugs and looters were out. We declined. It is now 8.25 p.m., and we must put out lights and go to bed. The last shock did the business. Not one of the girls will come near the house, so we must move across the hay to Oakland. We are ,going to store our furniture with some good friends who have offered us their basement for that purpose, and it is likely we shall find sleeping room with them. The people seem to be all on the move and the streets are like a fair, only there is not much mirth. The park is full of people tenting out and doing the test they can. Births from thirty to forty, and deaths I don't know how many. All this business has to go on, earthquakes and fi,-e notwithstanding. I am just resting a bit after a bard day packing for moving. My address for the present will be, H. WHITHASI c/o Mr. Stott, 2855, Jackson-avenue, Alameda, Park-street Station, Oakland; California.

The Examiner, Saturday, May 19, 1906. WOMAN'S REALM WOMAN'S SORE BACK.

DRESS-FASHION-HOME.

A SENSIBLE REST CURE.

Women fly to all manner of expensive and inconvenient rest cures nowadays, but if they would but realise it they might get the same results in their own homes under much more agreeable circumstances, declares "Miranda" in the Lady's Pictorial. It is coming to he recognised by many doctors that needlework is one of the best possible nerve-soothers, and that the old-fashioned forms of stitchery and other purely feminine handicrafts are the most suit. able for the purpose. There is nothing better, for example, than lace-making, which is a beautiful industry in itself, but another very pietuf:e6que pursuit which may be profitably indulged in by women who want to be rid of the jar and fret of modern life is that of spinning. A woman shews to great advantage at a spindle, and there is certainly, something very soothing about the quiet turning of the wheel and the slow but sure production of the thread from the seemingly impossible tangle of flax. , A TAILOR. MADE SIMI' BLOUSE.

Of the convenience and desirability of the plain tailor-rnz..—le shirt-blouse there can be little doubt. It is distinctly the smartest. thing for morning and country wear, while its excellent washing and wearing qualities make it one of the most economical of garments. An excellent example of the useful shirt is pictured in our sketch. The material of which it is fashioned is a plain White linen, coarse in weave, and not at all expensive. The front consists of a sort of plastron, shaped

ia the upper part something after the fashion ot a tabbed yoke. Beyond this come shaped tucks, and beyond again a plain tuck over each shonlder. Tucks and front are piped with dark- blue machine stitching in the same colour, and finished with large blue-and-white embroidered buttons. A navy-blue skirt, belt, and stock com- plete the neat and smart little morning rig-out. THE SIMPLER Lnrs.

On every side, says a writer in London Opinion, one hears that the lavish expenditure upon private entertainments so noticeable of late years will not be nearly so extravagant this season. Already a vogue for smaller and more select parties is growing up, and it is to be hoped that the day of big crushes is over. The King's aversion to long and heavy dinners has helped to introduce simpler fare, and though perhaps never have the delicacies of the table received more attention than at the pre- sent time, menus are shorter than they have ever been. Another prominent feature of the coming season's entertaining will be the brief invitations for all manner of parties. Modern hostesses prefer to do away with ceremony, and make ' their receptions smart and informal, and invitations are seldom issued more than a week in advance. Some of the gayest balls of last season were arranged for at a few days' notice.

SMART LINEN COSTUME.

In our sketch will be seen a neat costume of white linen. The smart little coat terminates with a short basque, and is drawn in at the waist with a belt of butcher-blue linen, the belt fastening beneath the loose fronts of the coat, which, in their turn, are adorned with a double row of pearl buttons, fastened across with loops of cord. There are revers of blue linen, and over them is,

depicted a dainty collar of broderie anglaise, !nislied with a frill of quilted muslin. The .leeves are full at the shoulder, but fit closely to the arm below the elbow, finishing at the wrist with a pointed cuff of blue linen. The skirt ii perfectly plain, being very full at the bottom—and stitched at the hem. The hat that completes the costume is a. French sailor shape, composed cn! 17:roderie anglaise, and trimmed with blue sill: ribbon to match the blue of the linen facings.

TO-DAY'S RECIPE.

SALT COD CREA3L—Soak a piece of salt cod for ; forty-eight hours, constantly changing the water. ' cook in cold water, and as soon as there are • signs of boiling, add a tumbler of cold water. Take the fish off the fire, and remove the lid fcr fifteen minutes. Then remove the cod; drain, bone, and skin it. Place .in a saucepan, sprinkle with lemon-juice, and stir . over the fire while someone pours some fine olive oil, drop by drol, until the cod resembles a thick paste and clings .to"? the saucepan while stirring. Add half a .sf milk the same nuantity of water. and :ontinue stirring. prop in some snore on, ann, when quite dissolved to a cream, season with ...hoped parsley and a, little garlic warmed in oil. sprinkle with salt and pepper, and serve very kol. To be perfect the cream must never boil over.

THE DUST NUISANCE.

Mr. A. J. King Asks a Question in

Parliament.

In the House of Commons on Tuesday, Mr. J. King, M.P., asked the President of the Local Govern- ment Board if his attention had been called to complaints by farmers in Cheshire and other agricul- tural counties of the damage and inconvenience which they suffered from the dust raised by the passage of motor-cars over the country roads in any weather, and whether be proposes to take steps to abate the nuisance. Mr. Burns said he had received many representations, not only from Cheshire, but from many other parts of the country, as to the vexatious annoyance arising thorn the dust caused by motor-cars. He had given instruc- tions that these communications should be laid before the Royal Commission on Motor-oars. CHESHIRE COUNTY COUNCIL'S ATTITUDE.

A report presented to the Cheshire County Council on Thursday is to the effect that there appears to be less inclination on the part of the urban authorities to experiment with any of the dust-laying materials at present in the market; the tendency is more in the direction of altering methods of construction to pre- vent or lessen the creation of dust. A sub-committee has decided that it is not expedient at present that the county surveyor should incur expenditure out of county funds in experiments to prevent dust raising on the main roads. but he is authq- rised to sanction the use by privat3 indi - vidnals of such materials as he may consider advisable. The clistrict surveyors have been instructed to arrange as far as possible for the brushing of the' main roads during dry weather, so as to minimize as much as pos- sible the nuisance arising from dust from ,the motor car traffic. SUCCESSFUL DUST EX:FERMENTS.

The Surveyor reported to Northvvich Urban Council on Tuesday night, that experiments with calcium chloride liquor had been remarkably successful as a dust preventative. Having regard to the duration of the applicationthe cost was less than ordinarystreet water- ing.

Sunday Scheel Anniversary.

The anniversary services in connection with the Stockton Heath Providence Sunday School (Indepen- dent Methodist) were held on Sunday, the preacher for the day being Mr. George Hunter, of Leigh. Special anthems were rendered by the choir and solos by Mr. E. H. Pearce who gave an able rendering of "The Lord is my Shepherd," and "Arm, arm, ye brave." In the afternoon a children's service was held, in which several of the scholars took a prominent part.. Thechait was occupied by Mr. R. B. Woods, of Latchford, and an address was given by Mr. George Hunter, upon the work of the Sunday school, Solos were rendered by Miss Mounfield and Miss Houghton. The cpllections amounted to £8 Bs.

MY DEAR CHILDREN, At last I am able to announce the winners of the competition- for the best account of your Easter holidays. I received so many letters from you all, and they were all most interesting, that I really had quite a difficulty to decide between them. However, I think the letter printed below from Master Harry Richards is the best, and I have sent him the first prize; the .second prize has been awarded to Miss Daisy Bakewell. I hope both these members will be_pleased with the award. I hope that all who have not been fortunate enough to win prizes thio time will try for the next Competition. You all stand equal chances, as if you add your age to all contributions, I will take this into considera- tion.

I hope to receive your ideas for coMpetitione for boys and girls. Do not delay in sending me this, as I must soon be making arrange- ments for our June Competitions. We are now getting warmer weather, so 1 dare say your plants are coming up nicely; you must keep me posted as to the progress they make, so that I can tell you when to send me a law specimens. You will find many interesting contributions, sent me by members, printed below. I find I am wanting a flies piece of poetry, so I will award a prize to the beet piece sent me for -next week's paper. With all good wishes from UNCLE NED.

Dear Uncle Ned,—l am writing to tell you how I spent my Easter holidays, and how long my garden is. I went on the Castle on Easter Monday, and I heard the Town Prize Band play. They started at three o'clock, and they played several songs for an hour and a-half. I then went up the Castle Keep, and I could see a long way, because the weather was pleasant. -On Monday night I went into the Public Hall, and they showed us the pictures of the "Great Train Robbery" and "The New Errand Boy." They were most realistic. I must tell you about my garden. It is about forty square yards in area. It is where the sun gets at it all day long. I set my seeds about a week ago, and I have had to water them three times on account of having no rain, but I expect they will be all right.

From your loving Nephew, ' HARRY RICHARDS.,

Dear Uncle Ned,—We broke up for our holi• days on Thursday dinner•time. In the after noon I went to market with mother. At nigh, I stayed at home and pled with my doll. On Friday morning I was up early ready • for the hot cross buns. When the postman came he brought several picture postcards, one of which was for me. The next Morning we had eiglu little chickens, of which my sister is very proud In the afternoon we all went to the cemetery and took some flowers. At night I went in tht study and finished "Fir Tree Farm:" The nexi .day was ,Sunday, and in the morning I stayed at home. In the' afternoon I went to Sunday School, and at night I went to Church. The next day was Monday, and in the morning I went a walk to Branstone, which was very nice. In many of the fields there were some: people playing golf. In the afternoon my mother, ' father, sister, and I went to Vv'inshill on a car. When we got off the car, we went down Mill ' Hill Lane, which was very pretty. Then we ' went down Newton Road, and got on a car, and went to the Station. Then we got on a Branstone Road car and went home. At night I had a friend come, and we stayed in the study. Tuesday I stayed at horne all day because_l was not very well. Wednesday morn- ing I tirade a pinafore for my doll. In the after- noon I had: two friends to tea. At night I „went a walk. The next day was Thursday, and in the morning I set some seeds in my garden. In the afternoon I went a walk with mether. In the evening I stayed at home and read a book which is called "A Stout English Bowman." Friday I stayed at home and played with my doll, owing to bad weather. The next day was Saturday, and in the morning I straightened my bag ready for school on Monday. Afternoon I stayed in and read a book, and at night 'I went to town. Sunday I went to Church.—l remain, yours ever, DAISY BAKtWELL.

THE LITTLE BIRD'S SONG.

A little bird With feathers brown

Sat einging in a tree; The song was very soft and low,

But sweet as it could be. And all the people passing by Looked up to see the bird, That made the sweetest melody That ever they had heard.

'Your loving Nephew, WILLIE JENKIN SON.

WEATHER WISE.

Sir Isaac Newton was once riding over Salisbury Plain, when a boy keeping sheep called to him, "Sir, you had better make haste on, or you will get a wet jacket." Newton, look- ing round and observing neither bleuds nor a speck on the horizon, jogged on, taking very little notice of the boy's information. He had made but a few miles when a storm, suddenly arising, drenched him to the skin. z:,urprised at the occurrence, and determined, if possible, to ascertain how an ignorant boy had attained a precision and knowledge in the weather, of which the wisest philosopher would be proud, he rode back, wet as he was. "My lad," said Newton, "I'll give thee a guinea if thou wilt tell me how thou cant foretell the weather so truly." "Will ye, sir? I will then!" said the boy, scratching his head and holding out his hand for the guinea. "Now, sir," having received the money, and pointing to his sheep, "when yOld see that black ram turn, his tail towards the wind, 'tis a sure sign of rain within an hour." "What," exclaimed Newton, " must t, iu order to foretell the weather, stay here and Ratch which way that ram turns his tail?" " Yes, sir." Off rode Newton, quite satisfied with his discovery. W. S.

A LITTLE HEROINE. Many years ago there lived in the North of Svntlancl, in a small fishing village,two little. whose names were Annie and Millie Grant, f.hey had to go to a large town, many milee . rem their home to school. Annie was nine years old, and Millie six. One day, when they ,vere at 'School a cry of "Fire" Was raised, and the children rushed panic-stricken from the ourning building. When they were all putside a cry wao heard. "Little Millie Grant Its still in' the burning ochool, who will go back and save her?" Immediately an agonising scream was 'beard, and all turned their heads to zee who it was. It was the cry of Annie Grant, aes she rushed into the burning school to save her little sister. She was soon seen returning from the flames, carrying her sister, and when she earns into the open air she fell senseless to the ground.

« * * * * Littls Annie had saved 'her sister, but she was ,o-terribly burned that the burns she received were fatal, and she only lived tO speak these • woids : 31.111 e af e ? BY THE CLUBMAN.

Cycling and Other Pastimes.

Much has been written to show the utility of the cycle in enabling those interested in other pastimes to get from and to their sports easily and quickly; but even those who use the bicycle for such pur- poses seldom take the trouble to get all the benefit from the use of the cycle which they,might obtain.

yers riding their cycles and carrying t• racquets in their hands, a practice at once awkward and dangerous. A shilling clip can be bought at any cycle shop enabling the racquet to be carried on the cycle as shown in the picture

- ,ement in

,

On the other hand, the caddy bag, with golf clubs, is undoubted lY bptter carried slung across the rider's back, as shown in the Sketch above, no completely satisfactory system of attaching the hag to the frame of the cycle being available as far as this writer knows.

Deferred Papnents. Dealing with the big output of cycles during the last eighteen months, largely due to the inbrease . in the credit system, the London 'Evening News' points out that very few people have considered what they have to pay for credit. One of the leading films in the trade advertises that there is no increase in their prices for ,credit, but that 10 per cent. is deducted for cash. \Now, assuming, as is the regular custom, payment is made in twelve monthly instalments, interest for that on the first month is at the , rate of no less than 120 per cent. per annum, GO per Cent, for that of . the second month, 40 per cent, for the third month, and so on, gradually, diminishing, until the normal rate of 1,0 per cent. is reached., Thus, . for example, the buyer of • £6 bicycle on credit not only loses the 12s. he would receive in discount for cash, but in the course of his monthly payments he drops another 10 per cent. For, if the credit buyer only paid 10 per cent. per annum for the _

convenience Of giallutil payment's, his disadvantage would only amount to Gs. 6d. on a £6 machine, as _cotn,pared with a cash pur;:haser, but as it is he wless-Gx. 61. more, or 12x. in all. What happens is, that. additional the credit buyer unconsciously pays an 10 per cent, per annum to the trader, or reeeiv: •ng and taking care of his instal- ment l until the total of the purchase-money is completed. All this i very fine moralizing, no doubt; but those who want to purchase a bicycle upon deferred payments generally agree that the small extra charge made for the accommodation is really not worth consideration, especially seeing that many Of the firms who do this business, like the New Hudson Cycle Co., for instance,, allow a rebate if the payments are kept promptly paid, up, and this makes the cost of credit in the end very little indeed.

LETTERS TO THE EDITOR.

NOTICE TO CORRESPONDENTS. Authors of letters intended for publication must in all cases send their names and addresses to the Editor as a guarantee of good faith.

Mr. Crosfied M.P., and Religious. Teaching. .

Sir,-1 have recently and again yesterday morning, received letter from some friends of mine in Warring- ton who are anxious about the question of Denomina- tional Religious teaching in schools, and amongst them are some Catholic friends, who, for that reason, asked sue to oppose the second reading of the Bill (for which I voted an hour ago). Without travelling over much of the ground that will be quite familiar to your readers, still as this Bill is of paramount importance, it may not be out of place to emphasise a few of the more important statements that have been made in the debate. First of all we have Mr Chamberlain's frank avowal that the Bill of 1902 gave cause for great dissatisfaction amongst the Non-con- formists, and that if in his opinion this dissatisfaction was exaggerated, still an amount of passionate resent- ment was aroused against this Bill, which, as he frankly says, would have compelled any Government to alter it. The two general principles of popular control and abolition of tests for teachers, subject to whatever qualification there may be in Clause 4 are the main principle and basis of the Bill in regard to the teaching of religion in schools. I cannot but think the import- ance of Clause 4 has been entirely under-rated by some of my friends who have written me. It has been remarked in the course of the debate that the power of a new democratic Parliament like that which has been called into existence—its power to make concessions in the interests of particular denomina- tians—is by no means unlimited. Quite true, but I venture to repeat that Clause 4 is a concession of great importance. and Mr. Birrell's most emphatic assurances that it is intended to be an absolute working reality make me very hopeful that the main additional concession in this connection, for which Mr. Redmond pleaded yesterday afternoon in a closely reasoned, forcible, and at the same time eloquent and impassioned speech will be conceded in the Committee stage. To make matters clearer perhaps I had better quote the opening lines of Clause 4. "4.—(1) A- load education authority may afford extended facilities for religions instruction of some special character not permitted under section fourteen of the Elementary Education Act, 1870, in any transferred voluntary school in an urban area,l by permitting the religious instruction given in the school to be instruction of that special character, notwithstanding anything in that section, if an Lapplication is made to them for those extended facilities, and if they are satisfied after holding a public local enquiry with reference to the applica- tion.— (a) that the parents of at least four fifths of the children attending the school desire those facilities; and (b) that there is public school accommodation in schools not a ffected by a permission given underl this section for the children attending the school whose parents do not desire those What Mr. Redmond and Mr. Dillon have urged is that in the first line the word " shall " be substituted for the word " may ;" an I am firmly convinced that if the cogent and eloquent speeches that have been delivered in this debate could have been delivered in Warrington there would have been few of any Denomination who would object to the clinching of the nail by that altera- tion : because it, will simply confirm in practice what Mr. Birrel I emphatically declares is his deliberate pur- pose, namely, that the teaching and, as it is called, the "atmosphere " in the Catholic, and Jewish schools too, shall continue as heretofore. Mr. Redmond in his speech this afternoon snoke of the causes which have led to so many very poor Irish Catholics earning their livelihood In England, and this is a fact which I think should and will appeal to all Englishmen who try and take a broad- minded end tolerant view of the question. Mr. Morley said in effect the other day that the claims of these pocir Irish Catholics had been specially considered in Clause 4. On the other hand it must be emembered that given the same Requisition by the parents of four- fifths of the children it is open to any Protestant denomination to demand denominational teaching on the same lines. Let us hope and believe that as a general rule the very large majority will do nothing of the kind ; that Protestants will make commea cause together, seeking not to sow sectarian difference and preindice in the minds of the children, but to avoid doing so. In conclusion, may I say that after listening to the main part of the debate this week, I feel that a great effort has been made to meet the claims and wishes of every class of the community. If the extremely iriport- ant concessions in the direction of denominational teaching that are offered now are not appreciated in the country, then I feel very certain the future alternative will not be in the direction of concessions to vet larger demands. Because if people see that real efficiency in the matter of Education is being sacrificed to sectarian bickerine, that will inevitably in the long run produce a re-action in favour of that secular teaching to which Mr. Chamberlain so significantly alluded in his enig- matical speech this week. Another curious allusion in that same speech of his was to his maiden effort in the House, delivered thirty years ago on the same subject, when he declared in the first place that" if priests and parsons would withdraw , their influence there would be no religious difficulty." Well, our clerical friends are no doubt as much entitled to divergence of opinion among themselves as are mere politicians, hut it is an encouraging sign to see how many enlightened men among them are nowadays anxious to assist in bringing about a peaceful settle- ment of this great and important question.—Yonrs.etc., A. H. CROSFIELD. London. May 11th, 190 G.

P.S.—T I may add one word personally it is to say that both this week I have to the best of my ability urged upon Mr. Birrell and other responsible men the above modificstion of Clause 4 in committee, and that some months since I did in the same wav all I possibly could as a new corner to the House to fulfil as T have fulfilled the nssuntnces in this connection which I gave during the election.

Sir,—Since I wrote von last Thursday night (or to be accurate, last Friday morning) I have read the speech delivered in Warring.ton by LORD HUDII CECIL. Needless to say that distinguished Statesman is missed on both sides of the House of Commons, where his brilliant intellectbas been universally admired. No abler critic of the Government's Education Bill could have been found to champion the cause of its opponents in Warrington. And yet if a careful comparison is made between Lord Hugh Cecil's speech and the speech delivered by Mr. Charles Parker at the annual meetings in connection with the Warrington Free Church Council. and to which you drew marked and most well deserved attention in your last issue, and, may I add, the com- prehensive. clear-sighted and admirable' letter which appeared in last week's papers from my friend, Mr.; George Walker, it will be clearly seen on which side the weight of argument in the true cause of Education lies, and how untenable Lord Hugh's position really is. These are the days of the "snapshots" of the London " ha'penny " morning papers on this sid of the "Herring Pond," and of men headlines which are often all that busy people find time to read, so they say, in the great Republic on the western side of it. But perhfips in view of the enormously momentous issue at stake it will not he overstepping the mark if one respectfully advises all interested in this Education controversy to read the two or three columns above referred to which fairly state the two sides of the question, subject to such concession as the Government is likely to make next week in committee. Already we are being threatened up here with the action of the Lords, and in order that the time aid money of the Nation and of Parliament should not be thrown away it is only too necessary that we have a strong backing of popular opinion determined to stand no nonsense and to see that this great effort of Mr. Birrell's should not be stultified and thrown away. Quoting a few lines from Mr. Wnlker's letter, he says "Surely the advantages offered by the Bill are are not to despised by the Churches. Itnrovides for religious teaching in every school. It allows definite religions teaching in Church schools two days a week. It leaves the Church in full possession of her schools. keens them in repair for her and Pays about ,E 500.600 annual rent for use of same. And vet the Bill is described as an Act of con- fiscation, spoliation and robbery by men who ought to know better to audiences who have not time Or disposition to form an opinion for theireselves. All this strife and bitterness is on the part of those who put the Church before the child, to secure or otherwise the privilege of teaching creeds and dogmas to the children of from four to fourteen Years of age in attendance at our elementary *schools. practically the children of the labouring classes." In the face of this how absurd to pretend that Mr. Birrell has not made a genuine effort to study the wishosand interests of the Established Church as for as it is possible for him to do so having regard to the general fundamental principles of the Bill. EDUCATION AND FREE TRADE. Turning to another matter, does it uot strike one as rather a pity that Some of our clerical friends .who did their best at the election to get rid of ":the evils of Free Trade "should not have improved the occasion of Lord Hugh's visit, by asking him for a little dissertation on the ceneral depression of trade in recent times, and Particularly in the great staple industry of Lancashire, the cotton trade, making special allusion to the short hours, which one would expect under this misguided Free Trade system, the slackness of demand, and the falling off in foreign exports. And if these friends of ours reply, "What on earth has that to do with Education? I think the connection is a little closer than they may imagine, for after all cheap prodrction has had everything to do -with the enormous and amazing development of the

Lancashire cotton trade, which, with the single excep- tion of agriculture I believe, affects the well being of the masses of our population (and therefore of their children) more than any other industry in the country. However much we may differ on other points in this educational controversy, there is I take it a general concensus of opinion that a good physical foundation is the first essential for really satisfactory mental development, that so I think a little homily from Lord Hugh on the advantages of cheap and plentiful food and clothing for the children, and what he, like the Liberal party, holds to be in other respects the immense advantages of Free Trade to us in England would have been peculiarly appropriate to the audience he was addressing in the Parr Hall.—Yours &c.. A. H. CROSFIELD, London, May 17th, 1906.

NOTES ON GAMES AND ATHLETICS.

Cricket.

Warrington have opened their season with a draw, this unsatisfactory result being the outcome of their match with Ashton-under-Lyne. In a sense they were lucky to be able to play out time whilst on the other hand there was the probability of ultimate victory had the match been concluded, as Bibby, the last man to take his stand at the wicket, was in a hitting humour, and when in such a vein he is capable of running up I a respectable score. Ashton batted first and scored I 120 runs, a by no means large total. Connor and Creswell were the most successful bowlers, taking respectively three wickets and four wickets for 39 runs scored off the deliveries of each.

When Warrington came to bat they shaped badly and four wickets In ere down for 27. It lay to Hawtin and Turton to improve matters and the score was carried to 73 before they were separated. Hawtin was res- ponsible for 25 placed to his credit as the result of patient cricket Turton also played careful cricket and had passed his colleague's score by two runs when he also was dismissed. Cresswell and Bibby made a stand as the last pair ,to be associated and when time put an end to the game they had carried the score to 101-22 less than the Ashton total.

The second eleven were robbed of a victory bv the lateness of the appearance of the Ashton-under-Lyne second contingent on the Arpley ground. The match started an hour late and when play closed Ashton with . seven wickets down were 27 arrear, with a total score of 47. For Warrington Wright and Dolan were the outstanding batsmen and scored respectively 18 and 24. Wright was evidently troubled by his injured ' hand—which placed him hors-de-combat all last season—but his exhibition was the best of the after- noon. Dolan was nervous at the commencement but when thoroughly set played a forcing game and scored freely. Ashton-under-Lyne were largely dis- missed by H. Callon who on Saturday's form deserves his place on the first eleven.

Widnes 2nd eleven received a severe shock when they visited the Walkers Brewery ground on Saturday. Batting first they scored 41, the last four wickets being taken without a run being scored. Walkers responded with 118 for seven wickets, Cain (48) Cook (30 not out) and Dargan (21) being the principal scores. Darg,an appears to be a useful all round man as with the ball he took three wickets at little cost.

Sankey Wire Mills' splendid victory over High Legh was la,r4gelv due to F. Burton who took six wickets for 15 runs. The other bowler also reaped a good harvest his four wickets costing only 20 runs. With the bat IL W. Cook headed the list of scorers with 23, followed by Whitfield (25), Taylor (13) and Hawthbm (10).

The brothers Taylor, who assist Moore and Dares- bury were in great form on Saturday taking between them nine wickets for 15 rims. Harry Taylor was the. most successful, dismissing six of his opponents and having seven runs scored off his bowling. As a result of such splendid work with the ball Latchford Baptists were only able to score 20 in reply to a total of 69.

The "Guardian " team Isi a closer fight with Lowton Parish Church, over whom they were victorious by 13 runs. The feature of the game was the batting of Hilton whose 25 was 6 higher than the score of Sankey, top scorer for the winning team.

Ten runs separated Penketh and West Derby 2nd, the Liverpool team holding the advantage. With the exception of Dumbell (19 not out) the Penketh team by no means distinguished themselves with the bat. On the attack U. Davies took four wickets at a cast of two runs each.

--- Widnes Secondary School were no match for the local secondary school and were easily defeated. None of their players reached double figures whilst Mee's 21 for Warrington was within two of their total score. Their failure was due to the excellent bowling of Whitehouse and Nield who were both in fine f rm.

To be dismissed for a total score of runs is exceed- ingly humiliating, but such was the fate of Widnes White Rose at the hands of Grappenhall. They could do nothing with the bowling of Watson who formed the nucleus of a good average by taking six wickets for one run. Grappenhall improved on their opponents' score by 60 runs.

Low scoring was the order in the match between Warrington Co-operatives and Penketh Friends' school, the scholars being defeated by eight runs.

As expected the first works competition was extremely exhilarating. Greenall Whitley batted fist and scored 37 to which Whitecross responded with 43. At ons stage the winners were four wickets down for' under ten runs but the later players adopted slogging tactics which enabled the team to win. To-night's (Friday) match is between Smethurst's and Cheshire Lines.

To-day Warrington enter upon their League pro- gramme, and St. Helens will he the visitois to the Arpley ground. The match should produce a keen struggle.

! HONESTY REMANDED. An English lady who had arrived at Calais by the , Dover steamer, bn coming out of the Customs House, I discovered to her consternation that she had lost her I handbag containing about £2OO in notes. She went to the police office to give information of her loss, and I whilst she was there a porter named Maillot came to ' the office with the bag, which he had found on the quay. . The lady rewarded him with £5.

The Examiner, Saturday, May 19, 1906

being the proud possessors for the first time of the Northern Union Cup,and in being champions of the South West Lancashire Leaguejon two wicasions. In addition to this, of course, they had to add the excellent success of the" A" team in their Combination and League matches. As he did not score any of the tries himself or drop any of the goals which contributed to their success in the past, they would understand that it was impossible for him to imagine that it was because he waspresident that they had done so well. (Laughter.) He had held the position of president for a long time and certainly considered it was time he retired. .They had many excellent supporters of the club who could well fill the position' and he was pleased to see that they had that night before them for president one of their oldest members, a staunch supporter of the club, an honoured and old official, and one who had occupied the office of secretary and also of their representa- tive upon the Northern Union League for some years. He was sure Mr. Warren would make a most excellent president being such an old and staunch supporter of the Warrington Football Club, and he hoped he (Mr. Warren)would occupythe position with their fullest con Hence and good wishes for years to come. In thanking the committee Mr. Neil said he thought one would have to go a very long way indeed to find a better Football Committee in the North of England. Their services were entirely given freely, and one and all were most enthusiastic and honest in their desires to benefit the club. He could assure them that though not their President his interest in the club would not diminish. He would do his level best to help wherever he possibly could or assist the officials in carrying out any schemes or work for the club's benefit—as it really would be impossible for him to forget the pleasure which be had had in being connected with the club. He sincerely hoped that it would be the pleasure of the new President to have the honour of regaining the Northern Union Cup at a very early date on behalf of the club. (Applause.)

WARRINGTON FOOTBALL CLUB.

ANNUAL MEETING.

Criticism of the Committee and the Northern Union.

There was a large attendance at the annual meeting of the Warrington Football Club which was held at the Co-operative Hall, Sankev-street, on Thursday evening under the presidency of Mr. W. R. Neil (retiring president). Amongst those present were:—Messrs. Henry Ashton (hon. secretary), T. Pemberton (hon. treasurer), T. Locker, T. Barnes. W. Hewitt, T. Bowers, T. Davies and W. Nevins (committee.) THE SEASON'S WORK. COMMITTEE CRITICISED. THE BATLEY CLIP MATCH.

The SBCRETARY'S report and treasurer's statement of accounts which appeared in last week's " Examiner " were submitted. _ . _

Mr. S. WRIGHT moved their adoption. He was sure that as far as the committee were concerned the past season had been a very arduous one and the position in which they were placed was a somewhat thankless one. There were many causes for the reduction of their balance by £3OO but he hoped the members would not attribute the falling off to any want of exertion on the part of the committee. In his opinion the blame did not lie with the officials of their club but primarily with the Northern Union. The attitude of the Union during the past few years, would in his view, cause plenty of clubs to be in a state of bankruptcy before long. Owing to the change of the league the first team had no fewer than five away matches in succession last season. Was it any wonder Ithat their gates went down and that interest began to wane? When they had so many away matches in succession defeat was generally the result and the spectators naturally began to fall off. Unless some drastic measures were taken by the Northern Union his opinion was that Rugby football would die out. When Mr. Warren was at the head of affaim the Union seemed to think more of the game. He tYought to-day the officials of the Union were simply looking after them- selves or their own clubs instead of looking to the wel- fare of the game. He was pleased Mr. Warren had been nominated as president of the Club and if he were elected he might be able to bring his influence to bear to induce the Northern Union to revert to the methods which made the game popular and kept their exchequer in a satisfactory state. Until they got men who would take an interest in the game for the games' sake Northern Union football would never prosper as it had done. He hoped they would not discredit the committee for the position the club was placed in. Never since he had been connected with the club had they experi- enced such a chapter of accidents as during the past season. The only point in connection with the working of the committee that he could find fault with was the selection of the team for the Northern Union cup match at Batley. (Applause.) They might have made an error in choosing the team. (Hear, hear). He btlieved that in cup-tie matches they wanted men in the team who had had practical experience of their positions and the club might perhaps not have lost close upon £3OO if a little bit more discretion had been exercised in the selection. He was not, however, going to condemn the committee for the sake of one error. He thought the officials had worked hard and, deserved the thanks of the members. (Applause.)

, Mr. A. Dcautoßinsfieconded. Mr. C. HotTaT said probably no one would differ from the report in regard to the result of the season's working but he thought they would with regard to the pauses to which the result was attributed. It appeared to him that for some years past the Warring- ton Club had got into a groove. During the first part of the season the team played most erratically and by the time half the season was over all chance of honours in the league had gone and they only seemed to pull themselves together for the cup ties. The past season had not been any different in this respect from its pre- decessors, except that the malady had been present in a more acute form. They were in the position of holders of the cup and they were looking forward to having some compensation in the cup-ties. They certainly thought they would not relax their hold on the cup without a stiff fight. When they saw the team which appeared in the field at Batley they were simply astonished and he thought they were entitled to some explanation from the committee. Unless they had an explanation they could not acquit the committee of mismanagement of the match. It might be said that it was not fair to point to a single match but the Batley match was the crucial one of the season and no experiments ought to have been tried for that match. (Applause.) They had had a most disastrous season and a strong effort ought to have been made to compensate for it. The match was the most disappointing they had had for years. They could have put in the field a team equal to any in the Union. (Applause.) He did not blame the players but the cemmittee who had placed them ,in their positions.
