ELECTION OF OFFICERS.

Alderman TrNsto:c and Mr. J. E. WNEREN had been nominated for the position of president of the club, but Mr. Ashton read a letter from Alderman Tinnion in which he said" I know of no position which 1 would value more highly but at the same time I think it undesirable that the office and honour should be put to competition but that it should be conferred by the unani- mousvote of the members.l also think it would be a mis- take to compete for the position against Mr. Warren. I 1 He is in many ways fitted for the position and I think it would be a suitable acknowledgement of his many valuable services rendered to football in general and to the good old club of Warrington in particular. Therefore I beg to withdraw my name but at some future time I shall be delighted to accept if the mem- bers again deem me worthy." (Applause). Mr. WARREN returned thanks for his unopposed election. He would do all he could to see that the club retained its present efficiency—nay more to see that it was restored to the position of emminence which it occupied twelve months ago. They would take for their motto a text which was hanging on the walls "They go from strength to strength." (Applause.) He hoped it would be possible ta bring about a working alliance with the old Rugby Union something on the lines of cricket with professionals and amateurs. When the split occurred it was urged against those who seceded that what they proposed would lead to pro- fessionalism and would Injure junior football. Those 1 who said that were right. In the rugby game they 1 were all suffering and were cribbed, cabined and con- fined for want of clubs and for his own part he would willingly assimilate the rules of the two Unions and , come to terms such as he suggested. In conclusion Mr. Warren thanked Alderman Tinnion for withdrawifig and I moved a vote of thanks to Mr. Neil for his services. (Applause.) I Mr. J. CRITCHLEY seconded and the vote was heartily I accorded.

INFIRMARY EXTENSION. Cycle Parade to be Held.

It will be remembered that at the annual meeting f Club there was a strong expression of the organization of o t the lN'ar r oi frigot roinD iCoyn ng a fancy dress parade i favour n aid of the Infirmary extension fund. The matter was referred to the committee who have now definitely decided to arrange a parade to be held about July 11th or 21st. The business will include the election of officers and appointment of Various sub-committees. It is expected that the arrangements will be taken up with great enthusiasm and that a highly successful parade witil be the result. ! A LEAD FROM PEARSON AND KNOWLES MEN. The following letter speaks for itself:— The Pearson and Knowles Coal and Iron Company Limited, Iron Department. Warrington, May 10th, 1906. George Dakin Esq.,hon. treas., Warrington Infirmary. Dear sir,— Warrington Infirmary Extension ' Since it became generally known that the Board of Management considered it necessary to increase the accommodation of the above Institution, much interest in the scheme has been manifested by the employees of the Pearson and Knowles Coal and Iron Company. During the past week or two, meetings have been held I in the works, to discuss proposals for raising a sum of money amongst the men, that would represent a fair contribution towards the £15,000 required. After due consideration, it was thought that Five Hundred: pounds would be none too much to ask from the large number of hands employed. And to make it , suggested that the contributions should be spread over fall as lightly as possible upon all concerned, it WAS a period of eighteen months ; and that all employs whose weekly wages are 20s. and over, should consent to the deduction of one penny per week, and all those whose wages are less than 205., to a deduction of one halfpenny per week, or one penny every two weeks. These deductions to be continued over a period of 18 months, commencing on Saturday, June 2nd, 1900. If trade keeps fairly good, this arrangement should pro- duce well over £5OO, but in any case it is safe we think to calculate upon this sum as a donation from the employs of the Pearson and Knowles Coal and Iron Company: At the meeting of the works Sick Club , Committee, where it was decided to ask the men to ) agree to the above scheme, the members were 1 enthusiastic in advocating the claims of the Infirmary upon the working-men of the town, and hopes were expressed with every confidence, that other works, both large and small, would make a special effort in aid of the fund required. It is known that similar means of raising subscriptions towards the maintenance of the Institution are in vogne in two or three works in the " neighbourhood, but this appeal of course, is for some effort over and above the usual annual subscription.— We are, dear sir, yours faithfully, CHAS. E. BUNCH, THOS. ATHERTON, (Works Managers).

DEATH OF MR. J. CHRISTMAS THOMPSON.

Another venerable and respected figure has been removed from our midst by the death of Mr. J. Christmas Thompson, for thirty years headmaster of the Warrington School of Art, who passed away at his residence 17, Bold-street, at 6.15 on Saturday morning. The deceased gentleman was very active for one of his advanced age—eighty-one years— and it was only dur'm the past few weeks that he had been seriously ill. The cause of death was senile decay. By his kindly and sunny disposition Mr. Thompson had endeared himself to a very wide circle and ,in the world of art he was particularly well-known and esteemed. His talents were of a very high order and it afforded him great delight when he saw others making marked progress under his tuition. Although the end was not unexpected, the news of Mr. Thompson's death was received with general regret in the town. His career had been an interesting and useful one. A native of Carlisle, he received his education at the Grammar School of that city. Decid- ing to take up art as a profession he went to the Royal Institution, Edinburgh,in 1813, to study under Sir William Allen, Mr. Thomas Duncan, and Mr. D. 0. Hill. From thence he proceeded to the New School of Practical Art in London and qualified for , the Art Master's certificates. It was in 1855 that he was appointed to the headmastership of the Warring- ton School of Art in succession to Mr. Collinsou. Under his care the institution made splendid progress. When he took over the duties the school was in Academy-place. It soon b,ecame necessary to have larger and better-lighted rooms, and in 1857 the removal took place to the Museum and Library, Bold- street. The change was followed by a great increase of students in the different classes, and in 1861 the school obtained the full complement of local medals (30), and five National Medallions in the succeeding National Competition; while Birmingham with its 902 students against Warrington's 147 was the only pro- vincial school out of 85 competing which obtained the same number. In 1860 89 provincial schools competed for the ten gold medals and Warrington did itself great credit by winning two. Birmingham and Glasgow with many more students on the books only obtained one each. The improved accommodation at the Museum eventually became I inadequate and in 1882 a movement was started for the erection of a new school of art with modern appli- ances, the late Mr. John Crosfield heading the subscription list with the handsome sum of £l,OOO. , The ladies of the special class, acting upon a sugges- tion of Mrs. Pickmere, formed themselves into a committee, and with the Art Department's grant of £5OO, the necessary fund was completed and the handsome building in Museum-street was opened. In 1885 Mr. Thompson retired from the headmastership and his pupils presented him with a token of respect in the shape of his portrait in oils,painted by Mr. H.John- stone, of Manchester. Mr. Thomas Birtles made the presentation and the portrait was subsequently placed in the Art Gallery where it hangs to-day. Mr. Thomp- son was also the recipient of a black and white sea- scape, by the late Mr. George Sheffield, and on July 26th, 1894, on his resignation as art teacher at the Grammar School, was presented with a beautiful silver tea tray from the boys (past and present) and with an illuminated book containing the names of the subscribers. It is a striking tribute to Mr. Thompson's conspicuous ability as an art master thatmany eminent men received their early training from him, including Luke Fildes, J. Warrington Wood. IL Wood. and E. F. Brewtnall. In 1898, Mr. Birtles called the attentionlof Mr. Thompson to a list exhibited in the present School of Art (where he had not been since his resignation), giving the names of a few of the former students, and this information induced Mr. Thompson to compile a complete list of the successful students, elementary and advanced, com- mencing from the establishment of the institution in 1853, to the date of his retirement in 1884. This record wasissued in book form, as well as on large papers, and a copy is exhibited in the entrance hall of the Museum. While enjoying his well-earned retirement Mr. Thompson pursued art as a hobby and many paintings and sketches remain as evidences of his great love for the pastime. Those who were privileged to receive instruction from him at the School of Art will cherish the memory of those days, for Mr. Thompson was never wanting in acts of encouragement am; kindness towards those who persevered He leaves a widow and daughter to mourn his loss. THE FUNERAL. The funeral took place very quietly at the Cemetery' on Monday morning. The Rev. E. C E. Carleton per- formed the last rites and amongst those present were Mr. Robert Dobson (the deceased gentleman's solicitor), Dr. Joseph (his medical attendant),Mr. Josiah Whittle (brother-in-law), Mr. Charles Pi erpoint,Dr. Bowden,Mr. John Geddas, Mr. G. S. Pendlebury (representing the School of Art), Mr. Charles Leigh (Messrs. T. and J. L. Tunstall, accountants &c.), Mr. John E. Birtles (representing his father, who was a very old friend of the deceased), Mr. J. Rymer Young, Mr. William Fletcher (Whitley), Mr. A. S. Wright, Mr. James Green (Manchester), and Mrs. Atkinson. On the coffin were two choice wreaths, one sent by Mrs. Thompson and the other by her daughter Miss Hylda Thompson. Other wreaths were forwarded by Mr. and Mrs. Geddes and family, Miss Lucy Geddes, Mrs. Cole, Mr. and Mrs. J. Foster Cutler, Mr. and Mrs. F. C. Herzog, Mr. and Mrs. W. Fletcher, Mr. Robert Dobson, The Misses Dobson, Miss Howson, Mrs. Atkin, the Misses Atkin, Mrs. Healey and "Mary," and Mr. and Mrs. Atkinson.

BEAUTIFUL WARRINGTON. Hints to Hoiieholders.

The following is a copy of a circular which has been, or is being, left, at every house in the borough by the Beautiful Warrington Society :—" To the inhabitants of Warrington. We all strive to keep our homes neat, clean, and tidy. Why not so keep our streets ? We all admit that frequently our streets present an untidy, dirty and unsightly aspect, and we !also agree that much of this is preventible. But What is everybody's business is nobody's business ' I seems to have been the general motto.. We want to ' change this, and so for the benefit of all, the Beautiful Warrington Society appeals to you to regard the street in which you, live in as, in a certain sense, your own, and as you care for your home so to care for your street. Let your street be cleaner, brighter, tidier, and more attractive than any of the neighbouring streets. For your guidance we submit some answers to the question How can I help ?' (I) By not throwing any rubbish from the house. into the street. (2) By seeing that no waste paper gets into the street from the house, and by destroying any waste paper found near the house. (3) By cultivating window plants. (4) By planting shrubs or flowers in front of your house if you have any spice. (5) By not throwing any orange peel, banana skin, &c., on to the pavement or into the street, and by usin,, your influence in preventing others from so doing. (6) By encouraging your children to join the juvenile branch of the Beautiful Warrington Society."

===== 0002601_19060519_art0105.txt | included=true | included =====
PROPOSED ANNUAL SHOW.

The present year is the first during which actual practical work is being done by the Beautiful Warring- ton Society, as the Society during the latter portion of last year was opgaged chiefly in the preliminary work. In order to induce the members of the Society to take additional interest in the work, it was decided at a Committee meeting on Monday evening to hold an annual show and the first will he held on Saturday, September Ist. Prizes will be offered for plants and flowers grown by members of the Society, and there will also be exhibits of the plants given to the children by the Society. A leading feature of the Show will be banging baskets and window boxes. This show will no doubt raise the spirit of emulation and do much to spread the popularity of the principles advocated by the Society.

===== 0002601_19060519_art0106.txt | included=true | included =====
HOW TO START A GARDEN,

After the successful meeting held in the Co-opera- tive Hall last week, on the occasion of the lecture by Mrs. Leo Grindon, it would not be out of place to give a few hints on how to start a garden. There are many people (-mite willing to do something to improve their surroundings, by sowing seeds, planting shrubs, or plants, such as stocks, asters, geraniums, etc., but who have not known how to start. These few hints are written with a view to encourage or instruct beginners. , The first matter to consider is the soil. In some 1 cases this may not have been disturbed for years (I know of many such so-called gardens), and if so, it should be well digged and manured, after which it can 1 be sown with seeds of annuals such as nasturtiums, calendula, China or Indian pinks, Frbnch and ei,frican marigolds, nemesia, mignonette, annual chrysanthemums, candytuft, Virginian stock, and many others, which the local seedsmen will supply in penny packets. Sow the seeds rather sparingly, and not too deep. Tnose who wish to purchase plants ready grown, can find a large variety offered for sale in the market, each market day, by the agent to the Society and other tradesmen, at very moderate prices. When going through the market on Wednesday, I found some specimen window-boxes, filled with plants, which I am sure will meet a great-felt want. I also noticed some plants of nepeta glechoma variegate, a very pretty plant for hang- ing baskets and hanging pots. The foliage is of a pretty green and silvery white. This plant grows so freely that in the course of a few weeks the basket or pot will become almost hidden beneath its trailing

stems. The younger leaves and the extremities of the trails are flushed with a pink tint, in addition to the green and white, and the presence of this tint enhances the beauty of the whole plant. It can be used for edging of window-boxes, when it will trail over the front and improve the appearance.

Special Services at 11111 Cliff e.

Special services will be held at Hill Cliffe Baptist. Chapel on Sunday, when the preacher will be the Rev. Jno. T. Griffiths, D.D., of Edwardsdale, America. Mr. Griffiths is a gentleman who has taken a great interest in the history of Hill Cliffe—the oldest Nonconformist Church in Britain—and last year contributed an able article on the subject to the Baptist Commonwealth." In this he pointed out that the antiquity of the Church can be judged from the fact that in the graveyard there is a tombstone with the date " 1357," and other stones the dates on which span the whole period of the Tudors on the throne of England. Written testimony, he said, could be produced to show the existence of the Church in the Elizabethian period. The visit of the rev. gentleman will arouse considerable interest in locaL Baptist Circles. Wycliffe P.S.A. The weekly meeting of the above Society NTSS held on Sunday afternoon, Mr. R. Mills presiding over a good ;gathering. He spoke upon "Being members one of another" and pointed out that individual service was I needed in carrying on good work. As every member of the human body was needed--the arm, the leg, the ear and the eye—so every faculty of the spiritual man was needed to make him healthy, morally and spiritually. Mr. F. J. Adcock of Manchester was the principal speaker, and he drew useful lessons from Elisba:s life. Elisha was a good man and a helpful man, and the speaker drew attention to the need for good men at the present time—men of character, consistency and grit. Our lives should be unselfish and helpful to the well- being of others. The Secretary read the lessons. Mr. Adcock led the prayer. Mr. W. H. Payton Mus. Bac. presided at the organ, and also rendered an organ solo. The Secretary asked the brethren to assemble in large numbers next Sunday to elect the best officers, for the carrying on of the P.S.A. work for the coming year: Bold-street Wesleyan Church. At this church on Sunday morning the Rev. Joseph Manning preached on" Some aspects Taking for his text the - 49 : they that worship Him nim _ _ in truth "he dealt with the s,etio and the ethi sides of worship. He pointed out that the aesthetica, side had its avdantages and appealed to many minds,but there was a danger of making the services an end in themselves instead of a means to an end. With

regard to the eithical aspect, it made the test of a man's reverence not whether he thrilled at painted windows, solemn music and so- forth,but whether he bowed his soul to justice,mercy and truth. The ethical idea of worship did not mean that it was of no consequence whether the services were hi good taste or not. A simple service might be beautiful. The service which was crude and slovenly did not point to grest spirituality but to want of deep reverence. This ideal involved the best that could be obtained in music and forms of worship—not, however, the best simply to please the artistic sense but because it was for God, and through the best man was most likely to reach God.—ln the evening the Rev. John Gunnell preached.

FORM 308 A (NOTICE). EDUCATION ACT, 1902. SECTION 8 (1) LANCASHIFE COUNTY COUNCIL. VOTICE IS HEREBY GIVEN in accordance with the provisions of Section 8(1)of the Education Act. 1902, that the County Council of the County Palatine of Lancaster, being the Local Education Authority for the purposes of Part 111, of that Act, propose to provide a new Public Elementary School for about 400 children at Penketh in the County Palatine of Lancaster. The School will be available for the following area : TOWNSHIP OF BOLD. TOWNSHIP OF CUEEDLEY. TOWNSHIP OF PENKETH. TOWNSHIP OF SANKEY. HARCOURT B. ()LAHR, • Clerk of the County Council. Dated 17th May, 1908. Section 8(1) of the Elucation Act, 1902, provides as follows : (1) Where the Local Education Authority or any other persons propose to provide a new public elementary school, they shall give publienotice of their intention to do so, and the Managers of any existing school, or the Local Education Authority (where they are not themselves the persons pro- posing to provide the school), or any ten ratepayers in the area for which it is proposed to provide the yahoo], may, within three months after the notice is given, appeal to the Board of Education on the ground that the proposed school is not required, or that a school provided by the Local Education Authority, or not so provided,as the case may 13., is better suited to meet to meet the wants of the district than the school proposed to be provided, and any school built in contravention of the decision of tide Board of Education on such appeal shall be treated as unnecessary."

THE EXAMINER. BUT 19th 1906.

The brighter side of political life is instanced in a sketch of Mr. F. E. Smith, M.P.,—who is well-known in Warrington as a clever barrister—which appears in this week's "World." By an interesting and decidedly piquant arrangement, the chambers he occupies on the second floor of one of the roomy red- brick buildings in Elm Court are shared with a mem- ber of the Ministelial majority, in the person of his I friend, Mr. Dudley Ward, the distinguished oarsman and junior Liberal member for Southampton. As regards facilities for informal "pairing," this partner- ship has obvious conveniences; and if the two I friends and political opponents ever find time to indulge in private debates on burning questions it may be taken for granted that they, are not conducted in such a manner as to involve the risk of any "scene in the house," or to imperil the ancient peace which broods over those sequestered Temple buildings. * * We wonder what sort of patriotism or Socialism it is that makes Keir Hardie, M.P., take the side of the Zulus in the present South African outbreak. He denounces the punitive efforts of the military and volunteers on the spot as "horrible butchery,"the most remarkable thing about which is that the Liberal Government "sat supinely by and allowed it to take place apparently without a word of protest." This is sheer drivel, in our opinion, and not worthy the leader of the hard-headed Labour party in the House If the Zulus were allowed to run riot there would be. some very real " horrible butchery " among the white settlers and their families, at which we suppose Mr. Keir Hardie would " sit supinely by." In days gone by we have robbed the Zulu of his land, and taxed his existence almost, but sympathy with him on that account should not blind our eyes to the fact that to- day he must be kept in subjection.

Dr. Reich on Cycling. Dr. Reich, whose lectures on Plato at Claridge's Hotel have excited much interest in society circles', is, evidently a believer in the benefits of cycling. "The bicycle," he says, "has been the greatest foe to the drink traffic It has accomplished infinitely more than all the talk of the teetotallers, and far more sanely and beneficially." Are Speed Gears Reliable? Many riders abstain from speed gears because they' have an idea that the complicated mechanism shown in the drawings illustrative of these gears issued by their manufacturers must be liable to get out of order. But as a matter of*fact ,quite the contrary is the case. It is really quite rare to find any one who uses 'a speed-gear who has any com- plaint to make of it. The makers of the Hub Two-Speed Gear publish a peculiar, letter from a cycle repairer, which bears rather on this question, and which 'hens as follows :—"Please send by return one new wire and fittings, same as enclosed, and we might mention our customer tells us he has ridden your hub 30,000 miles in four years, and it is now as good as 'ever. We ourselves have built the same hub into three different rims. Send by return and oblige." This is quite the general expe- rience, and no iyclist should refrain from availing himself of the advantages of any of the different makes of speed-gears because of any lingering suspicion of their liability to failure, for it is quite evident they are among the most durable and reliable parts of the cycle.
